Characterizing Implementability of Global Protocols with Infinite States and Data
Elaine Li, Felix Stutz, Thomas Wies, Damien Zufferey

Abstract
. We study the implementability problem for an expressive class of symbolic communication protocols involving multiple participants. Our symbolic protocols describe infinite states and data values using dependent refinement predicates. Implementability asks whether a global protocol specification admits a distributed, asynchronous implementation, namely one for each participant, that is deadlock-free and exhibits the same behavior as the specification. We provide a unified explanation of seemingly disparate sources of non-implementability through a precise semantic characterization of implementability for infinite protocols. Our characterization reduces the problem of implementability to (co)reachability in the global protocol restricted to each participant. This compositional reduction yields the first sound and relatively complete algorithm for checking implementability of symbolic protocols. We use our characterization to show that for finite protocols, implementability is co-NP-complete for explicit representations and PSPACE-complete for symbolic representations. The finite, explicit fragment subsumes a previously studied fragment of multiparty session types for which our characterization yields a co-NP decision procedure, tightening a prior PSPACE upper bound.



1.
Introduction

Concurrency is ubiquitous in modern computing, message-passing is a major concurrency paradigm, and communication protocols are therefore a key target for formal verification.
Communication protocols specify distributed, message-passing behaviors from a global point of view, altogether describing the interactions between all participants in the protocol.
Implementability and synthesis are two central questions to the verification of communication protocols.
Implementability asks whether a protocol admits a distributed implementation, and synthesis in turn computes an admissible one.
A distributed implementation is considered admissible if it is deadlock-free and exhibits exactly the same communication behaviors described by the specification.
We refer to the latter property as
protocol fidelity
.
The implementability question precedes the synthesis question in importance: synthesizing implementations for unrealizable protocols is a fruitless endeavor.

Global protocol specifications find industry applications in the form of UML’s high-level message sequence charts and the Web Service Choreography Description Language, and are widely studied in academia in the form of multiparty session types and choreographic programming.
Multiparty session types (MSTs) have been implemented in at least 16 programming languages
including Python
(
Demangeon et al., 2015
;
Neykova and Yoshida, 2017
;
Neykova et al., 2017
)
, Java
(
Hu and Yoshida, 2016
;
Hu and Yoshida, 2017
)
, C
(
Ng et al., 2012
)
, Go
(
Lange et al., 2018
;
Castro-Perez et al., 2019
)
, Scala
(
Castro-Perez and Yoshida, 2023
)
, Rust
(
Cutner et al., 2022
;
Lagaillardie et al., 2022
)
, OCaml
(
Imai et al., 2020
)
, F#
(
Neykova et al., 2018
)
,
and applied to
operating systems
(
Fähndrich et al., 2006
)
,
high performance computing
(
Honda et al., 2012
;
Niu et al., 2016
;
de Muijnck-Hughes and Vanderbauwhede, 2019
)
,
cyber-physical systems
(
Majumdar et al., 2019
;
Majumdar et al., 2020
)
, and
web services
(
Yoshida et al., 2013
)
.
We refer the reader to
(
Yoshida, 2024
)
and
(
Montesi, 2023
)
for a comprehensive survey of MST and choreography applicability respectively.

To model real-world verification targets, we desire for our protocol specifications to be as expressive as possible.
Various dimensions of expressivity have been explored in the literature, such as arbitrary message payloads, non-deterministic choice, unrestricted recursion and parametricity.
Formalisms such as choreography automata
(
Gheri et al., 2022
)
, high-level message sequence charts
(
Mauw and Reniers, 1997
;
Genest et al., 2003
;
Genest and Muscholl, 2005
;
Gazagnaire et al., 2007
;
Roychoudhury et al., 2012
;
Alur et al., 2003
;
Lohrey, 2003
;
Alur and Yannakakis, 1999
;
Muscholl and Peled, 1999
;
Morin, 2002
;
Genest et al., 2006
)
and session types
(
Honda et al., 2008
;
Bocchi et al., 2010
;
Bocchi et al., 2012
;
Toninho and Yoshida, 2017
;
Zhou et al., 2020
;
Li et al., 2023a
)
correspond to syntactically-defined fragments that incorporate a selection of these features.

In this paper, we study the implementability problem for a semantically-defined class of communication protocols, which we call
global communicating labeled transition systems (GCLTS)
.
GCLTS impose only modest syntactic restrictions and subsume many existing fragments of asynchronous multiparty session types and choreography automata.
GCLTS capture the following important features:

•

Asynchrony: the semantics are interpreted over a peer-to-peer, asynchronous network, with FIFO channels connecting each pair of protocol participants.

•

Generalized sender-driven choice: the only notable syntactic restriction imposed by our formalism is that at each branching point of the protocol’s control flow, a single participant chooses a branch.
In other words, the first message that is sent in each branch of a choice must come from the same sender. However, we impose no restrictions on the recipient or the message payload other than that no two branches share the same recipient and message.

•

Infinite protocol state: protocol states contain registers that take values from an infinite domain. This allows loops to carry memory across iterations, and allows the protocol to be specified in terms of dependent refinement predicates.

•

Infinite message payloads: messages can carry values drawn from an infinite data domain.

Implementability is undecidable for this general class of protocols.
The presence of and interaction between the aforementioned features means that even soundly approximating implementability is challenging.
Existing work is either comparable in expressivity but does not solve the implementability problem, or solves the implementability problem but is incomparably restricted in expressivity.

Zhou et al. (2020)
present a framework for synchronous, refined multiparty session types that soundly approximates implementability through its endpoint projection, but that may yield local specifications that are not implementable.
Several works
(
Alur et al., 2005
;
Lohrey, 2003
;
Stutz, 2023
;
Li et al., 2023a
)
precisely characterize implementability for finite protocol specifications. However, the implementability check in
(
Alur et al., 2005
;
Li et al., 2023a
)
relies on synthesizing an implementation upfront, which is not possible for infinite state protocols.

Das and Pfenning (2020)
study local session types with arithmetic refinements in a binary setting.

We address these challenges by decomposing the implementability problem into two steps.
First, we give a precise, semantic characterization of implementability for GCLTS that we prove sound and complete once and for all.
Our characterization is defined directly on the global specification, and thus forgoes the need to first synthesize a candidate implementation.
Moreover, our characterization gives a unified semantic explanation to disparate causes of non-implementability that arise from the expressivity of our protocol fragment.
We encapsulate the complexities introduced by communication-specific features such as asynchrony and partial information in the first step.
Our semantic characterization reduces implementability to (co)reachability in the GCLTS.
Specifically, we provide a sound and complete reduction to the first-order fixpoint logic
\mu
CLP
(
Unno et al., 2023
)
.
The
\mu
CLP calculus can express recursive predicates with least and greatest fixpoint semantics where the predicate body is constrained by a first-order logic formula over a background theory.
Our implementability characterization can therefore be checked by existing
\mu
CLP solvers.
Second, we use this reduction to obtain a
blueprint for solving implementability algorithmically.
Our reduction yields algorithms that are sound and complete relative to an assumed oracle for solving
\mu
CLP validity, in addition to decision procedures with optimal complexity for various decidable classes.

Contributions.

In summary, our contributions are:

•

Global communicating labeled transition systems (GCLTS): a semantically-defined class of asynchronous communication protocols that subsumes most formalisms in the literature.

•

A precise characterization of implementability for GCLTS.

•

The first symbolic algorithm for checking implementability of infinite, symbolic protocols that is sound and relatively complete.

•

Optimal decision procedures for checking implementability of finite protocols. In particular, we show that for explicit protocol representations that enumerate all states and transitions, the problem is co-NP-complete, and for symbolic protocol representations that encode states and transitions using predicates and variables, the problem is PSPACE-complete.

•

As a corollary of the previous result, we obtain a co-NP decision procedure for implementability of global types, tightening a prior PSPACE upper bound
(
Li et al., 2023a
;
Li et al., 2023b
)
.

2.
Overview

We motivate our work using an infinite state version of a two-bidder protocol, depicted as a high-level message sequence chart (HMSC) in
Fig.

2
. The protocol specifies the behavior of two bidders,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{1}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{2}}}
, who negotiate to split the purchase of a book from seller
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{S}}
.

The protocol begins with
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{1}}}
announcing to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{S}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{2}}}
the book
y
it proposes to buy. The protocol requires that
y
signifies a valid ISBN number, which we abstract with the predicate
\mathsf{ISBN}(y)
. The seller
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{S}}
then informs
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{1}}}
the requested book’s price
z
. After this,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{1}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{2}}}
enter a bidding phase in which they negotiate the split of their respective contributions
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
towards the purchase. In each round of the bidding phase,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{1}}}
proposes its contribution
b_{1}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{2}}}
. Bidder
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{2}}}
then decides to either abort the protocol by sending a
\mathsf{quit}
message to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{S}}
, or respond to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{1}}}
with its own bid
b_{2}
. In case
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{2}}}
aborts,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{S}}
echoes the abort message to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{1}}}
and the protocol terminates. In case
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{2}}}
continues bidding, if the sum of the proposed bids exceeds the book’s price,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{1}}}
informs
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{S}}
of the successful negotiation. Seller
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{S}}
in turn relays the message to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{2}}}
. Otherwise,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{1}}}
sends a
\mathsf{cont}
message to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{2}}}
, informing them that they need to enter another bidding round. Throughout the bidding phase,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{1}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{2}}}
track the values of their latest bids in the registers
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
. The refinements ensure that the proposed bids are strictly increasing from one round to the next, thus enforcing termination.

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{S}}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{1}}}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{2}}}
y\{\mathsf{ISBN}(y)\}

z\{z>0\}

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{S}}

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{2}}}

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{S}}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{1}}}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{2}}}
\mathsf{quit}
\mathsf{quit}

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{S}}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{1}}}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{2}}}
\mathsf{succ}\{b_{1}+b_{2}\geq z\}
\mathsf{succ}

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{S}}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{1}}}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{2}}}
b_{2}\{b_{2}>z_{2}\}

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{S}}

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{2}}}

\langle z_{1},z_{2}\rangle\coloneq\langle b_{1},b_{2}\rangle

.

Figs.

2
,
4
, and
4
show an admissible implementation for the two-bidder protocol in
Fig.

2
, consisting of a local implementation for each participant:
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{S}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{1}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{2}}}
.
The transition labels specify their local behaviors:

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{1}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{S}}!y\{\mathsf{ISBN}(y)\}

specifies that
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{1}}}
sends a message
y
to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{S}}
such that
y
satisfies
\mathsf{ISBN}(y)
, i.e.
y
is a valid ISBN number;

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{S}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{1}}}?y\{\mathsf{ISBN}(y)\}

specifies that
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{S}}
receives
y
from
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{1}}}
, and can assume
\mathsf{ISBN}(y)
holds of
y
.
We assume an asynchronous setting in which every pair of participants is connected by a FIFO channel.
The implementability of
Fig.

2
is witnessed by

Figs.

2
,
4
, and
4
, which
together exhibit the same behaviors as the global protocol and is never stuck.

To see that the implementability problem is non-trivial, consider a variant of the protocol in
Fig.

2
where the
\mathsf{succ}
message to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{S}}
is sent by
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{2}}}
instead of
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{1}}}
.
The resulting protocol is no longer implementable because
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{2}}}
never learns about the price
z
of the book
y
and is therefore unable to determine when the negotiation with
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{1}}}
has succeeded.

Our example highlights several important expressive features of GCLTS:

•

Generalized sender-driven choice: after
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{2}}}
receives a bid from
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{1}}}
, it has the option to either send a bid back to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{1}}}
and continue the bidding process, or terminate the protocol by sending a
\mathsf{quit}
message to the bookseller, who then relays the termination message to the first bidder. Due to this choice interaction alone, the protocol is not expressible in
(
Zhou et al., 2020
)
.

•

Infinite state: the protocol state contains registers that can be assigned values from an infinite domain. Registers are updated to store the last bid from each round
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
, and to enforce that bidders make strictly increasing bids per round.

•

Infinite message data: message payload values can be drawn from an infinite data domain, such as the book price
z
and bids
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
.

•

Dependent refinement predicates: message payloads are constrained by data refinements such as
z_{1}<b_{1}
and
z<b_{1}+b_{2}
. The refinement predicates can refer to current register values in addition to data values sent in prior messages.

•

Partial information: each protocol participant only has a partial view of the global protocol state. For example, even though
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{S}}
participates in the bidding phase of the protocol, it never learns about the bids
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
in each bidding round. In fact, the registers
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
that store the last bid are known only to the bidders.

The presence of these features in the class of communication protocols we consider makes checking implementability uniquely challenging.
For protocols with finite GCLTS specifications, deciding implementability in the presence of asynchrony and non-deterministic choice already presents a challenge.
Note that finiteness here refers only to the specification, and does not mean that the underlying protocol is finite-state, nor that it contains only finite traces.
Most existing work has therefore focused on developing projection operators that are sound but incomplete
(
Honda et al., 2008
;
Coppo et al., 2015
;
Toninho and Yoshida, 2017
;
Scalas et al., 2017
)
.
These projection operators solve implementability and synthesis simultaneously by computing a candidate implementation, but often fail eagerly for protocols for which an implementation exists.

Li et al. (2023a)
proposed the first sound and complete projection operator for finite, asynchronous, multiparty session types.
The projection operator critically relies on the observation that if a global type is implementable, then a canonical implementation implements it.
Thus, the implementability problem reduces to checking whether this canonical implementation indeed implements the global type, i.e. it recognizes the same set of behaviors and is deadlock-free.
Towards these ends,
Li et al. (2023a)
identify sound and complete conditions, referred to as
Send Validity
and
Receive Validity
, that are checked on the states of the canonical implementation.

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!:\!x\{\top\}
ⓐ

{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\!:\!y\{{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}y>x}\}
\raisebox{-.8pt}{b}⃝

{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\!:\!y\{{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}y=x+2}\}
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{b}}\{even(x)\}
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathsf{m}}\{odd(x)\}
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\!:\!z_{1}
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\!:\!z_{2}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!:\!z\{z=z_{1}-z_{2}\}
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\!:\!z_{2}
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\!:\!z_{1}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!:\!z\{z=z_{2}-z_{1}\}

Figure 5
.

Two protocols:

\mathbb{S}_{1}
using
ⓐ
with receive order violation

\mathbb{S}_{1}^{\prime}
using
\raisebox{-.8pt}{b}⃝
without receive order violation.

In the presence of dependent refinement predicates, checking these conditions is not straightforward.
Consider the examples
\mathbb{S}_{1}
(using
ⓐ
) and
\mathbb{S}_{1}^{\prime}
(using
\raisebox{-1pt}{b}⃝
) in
Fig.

5
, which are variations of the examples for Receive Validity
(
Li et al., 2023a
)
featuring dependent predicates.
A transition label
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\!:\!y\{y>x\}
, which is
ⓐ
for
\mathbb{S}_{1}
, atomically specifies the send event by
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
and the corresponding receive event by
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
, along with the constraint that
y
satisfies
y>x
.
In
\mathbb{S}_{1}
, participant
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
chooses a branch without explicitly informing
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
of their choice.
In both branches,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
is required to subtract the second value that is sent from the first value that is sent, and send the result back to
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
.
However, due to asynchrony, both messages can arrive in
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
’s message channels simultaneously, and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
cannot tell which value was sent first.
Therefore,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
may subtract the values in the wrong order, rendering the protocol unimplementable.

Li et al. (2023a)
propose one method of protocol repair: introducing a message sent by
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
on each branch that creates a causal dependency between the messages from
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}
, so that
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
can no longer receive them in either order.
The incorporation of dependent refinements enables a new method of protocol repair; one that does not change the communication events among the participants.
The newly repaired protocol is depicted in
\mathbb{S}_{1}^{\prime}
, in which
the predicate on the second transition is changed from
y>x
to
y=x+2
.
Despite the fact that
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
is still not informed of
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
’s choice,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
can
infer

{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
’s choice through the parity of the first value it received from
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
and thus correctly follow the protocol: if
y
is even,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
receives from
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
first, and if
y
is odd,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
receives from
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}
first.

q_{1}
q_{2}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{b}}
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!:\!x_{1}\{x_{1}=4\}
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\mathsf{o}}
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathsf{m}}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathsf{m}}
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!:\!x_{2}\{\top\}
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{b}}\{x_{2}\neq 5\}
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathsf{m}}\{x_{2}=5\}
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\mathsf{o}}

Figure 6
.

\mathbb{S}_{2}
: An protocol with a send violation.

We now turn our attention to send violations.
In the protocol shown in
Fig.

6
,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}
chooses a branch and communicates its choice to
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}
.
Participant
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
is again not explicitly informed of the choice: in fact,
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
can receive
4
from
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}
on both branches.
At first glance, it appears as though it is safe for
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
to send
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\mathsf{o}}
to
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}
upon receiving 4 from
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}
, because whilst
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
cannot distinguish the two branches, both branches contain the transition
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\mathsf{o}}
.
Upon closer inspection, the predicate guarding the transition immediately preceding
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\xrightarrow{}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}:{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\mathsf{o}}
on the lower branch,
x_{2}=5
, is only satisfied when
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
receives
5
from
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}
.
When
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
receives
4
, the lower branch from
q_{2}
is disabled, and since the upper branch from
q_{2}
does not contain the transition
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\mathsf{o}}
, the protocol is not implementable.

The examples above exemplify the ways in which refinement predicates complicate implementability checking for symbolic protocols. We return to these examples, in addition to some others, in greater detail in
Section

4
when we present our precise characterization of implementability.
We structure the rest of the paper as follows.
§
3
presents relevant preliminary definitions and defines the class of communication protocols we consider.

Section

4
presents our semantic characterization of implementability for GCLTS in terms of (co)reachability, and proves that it is precise.

Section

5
describes our sound and complete reduction from the characterization in
Section

4
to logical formulas in
\mu
CLP
(
Unno et al., 2023
)
, and additionally presents improved complexity results under certain finiteness assumptions on the GCLTS.

Section

6
discusses related work and concludes.

3.
Preliminaries

We introduce some basic concepts and notation before defining our class of protocols.

Words.

Let
Σ
be an alphabet.

Σ^{*}
denotes the set of finite words over
Σ
,
Σ^{\omega}
the set of infinite words, and
Σ^{\infty}
their union
Σ^{*}\cup Σ^{\omega}
.
A word
u\in Σ^{*}
is a
prefix
of word
v\in Σ^{\infty}
, denoted
u\leq v
, if there exists
w\in Σ^{\infty}
with
u\cdot w=v
;
we denote all prefixes of
u
with
\operatorname{pref}(u)
.
Given a word
w=w_{0}\ldots w_{n}
, we use
w[i]
to denote the i-th symbol
w_{i}\in Σ
, and
w[0..i]
to denote the subword between and including
w_{0}
and
w_{i}
, i.e.
w_{0}\ldots w_{i}
.

Message Alphabets.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a finite set of participants and
\mathcal{V}
be a (possibly infinite) data domain.
We define the set of
synchronous events

\Gamma_{\negthinspace\mathit{sync}}\coloneq\{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!:\!m\mid{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}∈\mathcal{P}\text{ and }m∈\mathcal{V}\}
where
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!:\!m
denotes a message exchange of
m
from sender
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
to receiver
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}
.
For a participant
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}
, we define the alphabet
\Gamma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}=\{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!:\!m\mid{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\in\mathcal{P},\;m\in\mathcal{V}\}\cup\{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!:\!m\mid{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\in\mathcal{P},\;m\in\mathcal{V}\}
, and a homomorphism
{\Downarrow}_{\Gamma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
, where
x{\Downarrow}_{\Gamma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}=x
if
x\in\Gamma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
and
\varepsilon
otherwise.
A synchronous event is split into a send and receive event for the respective participant, yielding
asynchronous events
.
For a participant
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}
, we define the alphabet

Σ_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}},!}=\{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}!m\mid{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\in\mathcal{P},\;m\in\mathcal{V}\}
of
send
events
and the alphabet

Σ_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}},?}=\{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\triangleleft{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}?m\mid{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\in\mathcal{P},\;m\in\mathcal{V}\}
of
receive
events.
The event
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}!m
denotes participant
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
sending a message
m
to
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}
,
and
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\triangleleft{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}?m
denotes participant
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
receiving a message
m
from
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}
.
We write
Σ_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}=Σ_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}},!}\cup Σ_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}},?}
,

Σ_{!}=\bigcup_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}}Σ_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}},!}
, and

Σ_{?}=\bigcup_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}}Σ_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}},?}
.
Finally,
Σ_{\mathit{async}}=Σ_{!}\cup Σ_{?}
.
We define a homomorphism to map the synchronous alphabet to its asynchronous counterpart, defined as

\texttt{split}({{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!:\!m)\coloneq{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}!m.\,{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\triangleleft{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}?m
.
Because
split
is injective, there exists a unique inverse, which we denote
\texttt{split}^{-1}
.
We say that
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
is
active
in
x\in Σ_{\mathit{async}}
if
x\in Σ_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
For each participant
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}
, we define a homomorphism
{\Downarrow}_{Σ_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
, where
x{\Downarrow}_{Σ_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}=x
if
x\in Σ_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
and
\varepsilon
otherwise.
We write
\mathcal{V}(w)
to project the send and receive events in
w
onto their messages.
We fix
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{V}
in the remainder of the paper.

Labeled Transition Systems.

A
labeled transition system
(LTS) is a tuple

\mathcal{S}=(S,\Gamma,T,s_{0},F)

where

S
is a set of states,
\Gamma
is a set of labels,

T
is a set of transitions from
S\times\Gamma\times S
,

F\subseteq S
is a set of final states,
and
s_{0}\in S
is the initial state.
We use
p\xrightarrow{\alpha}q
to denote the transition
(p,\alpha,q)\in T
.
Runs and traces of an LTS are defined in the expected way.
A run is
\emph{maximal}
if it is either finite and ends in a final state, or is infinite.
The language of an LTS
\mathcal{S}
, denoted
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S})
, is defined as the set of maximal traces.
A state
s\in S
is a
deadlock
if it is not final and has no outgoing transitions.
An LTS is
deadlock-free
if no reachable state is a deadlock.
Given an LTS
\mathcal{S}=(S,\Gamma,T,s_{0},F)
and a state
s\in S
, we use
\mathcal{S}_{s}
to denote the LTS obtained by replacing
s_{0}
with
s
as the initial state:
(S,\Gamma,T,s,F)
.

3.1.
Global Communicating Labeled Transition Systems (GCLTS)

We use LTS over the synchronous alphabet
\Gamma_{\negthinspace\mathit{sync}}
to model communication protocols from a global perspective.
We impose three more conditions on the class of LTSs we consider:
that final states do not contain outgoing transitions, that multiple outgoing transitions from a state share a sender, and that the LTS is deadlock-free.

Definition 3.1 (Global communicating LTS).

An LTS
\mathcal{S}=(S,\Gamma_{\negthinspace\mathit{sync}},T,s_{0},F)
is a
global communicating labeled transition system
(GCLTS) if the following conditions hold:

(1)

sink-finality
:
for every final state
s\in F
, there does not exist
l\in\Gamma_{\negthinspace\mathit{sync}}
and
s^{\prime}\in S
with
s\xrightarrow{l}s^{\prime}\in T
;

(2)

sender-driven choice
:
for all states
s,s_{1},s_{2}\in S
and
l_{1},l_{2}\in\Gamma_{\negthinspace\mathit{sync}}
such that

s\xrightarrow{l_{i}}s_{i}\in T
for
i\in\{1,2\}
,
there is a participant
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}

who is the sender for both, i.e.
\texttt{split}(l_{i})\in Σ_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}},!}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
, and furthermore
l_{1}=l_{2}\implies s_{1}=s_{2}
;

(3)

deadlock freedom
:
\mathcal{S}
is deadlock-free.

Condition
1
is ubiquitous in the domain of multiparty session types and was also shown to require special treatment in the literature on high-level message sequence charts
(
Dan et al., 2010
)
.

Condition
2
is a generalisation of most multiparty session types fragments, which require not only a dedicated sender but also a dedicated receiver.
This more restrictive condition is called
directed choice
.
In contrast,
mixed choice
lifts all restrictions on choice, and amounts to only requiring determinism.

Lohrey (2003)
showed that
implementability
is undecidable for HMSCs satisfying determinism and Condition
3
.

Stutz (2024)
showed that implementability remains undecidable for mixed choice global
multiparty session types satisfying determinism and Conditions
1
and
3
.
Sender-driven choice thus represents a good middle ground, allowing to express interesting communication patterns while retaining decidability of implementability.

Condition
3
simply requires that protocols do not specify deadlocking behaviors.

In the remainder of the paper we refer to a GCLTS simply as a
protocol
.

Restricting Protocols to Participants.

From a protocol
\mathcal{S}
, we can define a local protocol for each participant
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
via domain restriction to
Σ_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
.
Formally, given a protocol
\mathcal{S}=(S,\Gamma_{\negthinspace\mathit{sync}},T,s_{0},F)
,
we define

\mathcal{S_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\coloneq(S,\Gamma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\uplus\{\varepsilon\},T_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}},s_{0},F)

where

T_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\coloneq\{s\xrightarrow{l{\Downarrow}_{\Gamma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}s^{\prime}\mid s\xrightarrow{l}s^{\prime}\in T\}

for a participant
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}
.

Asynchronous Protocol Semantics.

Note that a protocol is specified using the synchronous alphabet
\Gamma_{\negthinspace\mathit{sync}}
.
To define the
asynchronous
semantics of a protocol
\mathcal{S}
we first map finite and infinite words of
\mathcal{S}
onto their asynchronous counterpart using
split
, thus obtaining a set of asynchronous words in which matching send and receive events are adjacent to each other.
In an asynchronous, FIFO network, two events are independent if they are not related by the
happened-before
relation
(
Lamport, 1978
)
.
For example, any two send events from distinct senders are independent.
Consequently, two words are
indistinguishable
if any asynchronous, FIFO implementation that recognizes one word must recognize the other, e.g.

w\cdot{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}!m\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}!m^{\prime}\cdot u

and

w\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}!m^{\prime}\cdot{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}!m\cdot u
, where

{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}≠{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
.
We define our protocol semantics as the set of
channel-compliant

(
Majumdar et al., 2021a
)
words that are closed under this notion of indistinguishability.
Channel compliance characterizes words that respect FIFO order, i.e. receive events appear after their matching send event, and the order of receive events follows that of send events in each channel.

Definition 3.2 (Channel compliance).

Let
w\in Σ_{\mathit{async}}^{\infty}
. We say that
w
is
channel-compliant
if for all prefixes
w^{\prime}\leq w
, for all
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\neq{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\in\mathcal{P}
,

\mathcal{V}(w^{\prime}{\Downarrow}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\triangleleft{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}?\_})\leq\mathcal{V}(w^{\prime}{\Downarrow}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}!\_})
.

The asynchronous semantics of a protocol is defined as follows:

\displaystyle\small\mathcal{C}^{\sim}(\mathcal{S})=\;

\displaystyle\{w^{\prime}\!\in\!Σ_{\mathit{async}}^{*}\mid\exists w\in Σ_{\mathit{async}}^{*}.\,w\!\in\!\texttt{split}(\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}))\land w^{\prime}\text{ is channel-compliant }

\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt\land\forall{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!\in\!\mathcal{P}.~w^{\prime}{\Downarrow}_{Σ_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!=\!w{\Downarrow}_{Σ_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}

\displaystyle\cup{}\;

\displaystyle\{w^{\prime}\!\in\!Σ_{\mathit{async}}^{\omega}\!\mid\exists w\in Σ_{\mathit{async}}^{\omega}.\,w\!\in\!\texttt{split}(\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}))\land\forall v^{\prime}\leq w^{\prime}.~\exists u,u^{\prime}\in Σ_{\mathit{async}}^{*}.~

\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188ptv^{\prime}\cdot u^{\prime}\text{ is channel-compliant}~\land~u\leq w~\land\forall{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}.~(v^{\prime}\cdot u^{\prime}){\Downarrow}_{Σ_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}=u{\Downarrow}_{Σ_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\kern 5.0pt.

Membership of infinite words is defined in terms of their prefixes: every prefix
v^{\prime}
must be channel-compliant, and moreover extensible to a word
v^{\prime}u^{\prime}
that is indistinguishable from another prefix already in the language.
Since we do not make any fairness assumptions on scheduling, the semantics of infinite words includes traces such as

({{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}!m)^{ω}

for the protocol

({{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}!m.{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\triangleleft{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}?m)^{ω}
, where only the sender is scheduled.
Membership of finite words follows standard MSC semantics.

In the remainder of the paper, we overload notation and use
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S})
to denote
\mathcal{C}^{\sim}(\mathcal{S})
.

Communicating LTS

We use communicating LTS to model the local behaviors of participants:

\mathcal{T}=\{\!\!\{T_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\}\!\!\}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
is a
communicating labeled transition system
(CLTS) over
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{V}
if

{T}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}

is a deterministic LTS
over
Σ_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
for every
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}
, denoted by

(Q_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}},Σ_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}},\delta_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}},q_{0,{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},F_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}})
.
Let

\prod_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}}Q_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}

denote the set of global states and

\mathsf{Chan}=\{({{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}})\mid{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\in\mathcal{P},{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\neq{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\}

denote the set of channels.
A
configuration
of
\mathcal{A}
is a pair
(\vec{s},\xi)
, where
\vec{s}\,
is a global state and

\xi:\mathsf{Chan}\rightarrow\mathcal{V}^{*}

is a mapping from each channel to a sequence of messages.
We use
\vec{s}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
to denote the state of
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
in
\vec{s}
.
The CLTS transition relation, denoted
\rightarrow
, is defined as follows.

•

(\vec{s},\xi)\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}!m}(\vec{s}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{s}^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})
if

(\vec{s}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}!m,\vec{s}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{s}^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}})\in\delta_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
,

\vec{s}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}=\vec{s}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{s}^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
for every participant
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\neq{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
,

\xi^{\prime}({{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}})=\xi({{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}})\cdot m
and
\xi^{\prime}(c)=\xi(c)
for every other channel
c\in\mathsf{Chan}
.

•

(\vec{s},\xi)\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\triangleleft{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}?m}(\vec{s}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{s}^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})
if

(\vec{s}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\triangleleft{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}?m,\vec{s}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{s}^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}})\in\delta_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}
,

\vec{s}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}=\vec{s}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{s}^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
for every participant
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\neq{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}
,

\xi({{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}})=m\cdot\xi^{\prime}({{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}})

and
\xi^{\prime}(c)=\xi(c)
for every other channel
c\in\mathsf{Chan}
.

In the initial configuration
(\vec{s}_{0},\xi_{0})
, each participant’s state in
\vec{s}_{0}
is the initial state
q_{0,{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
of
A_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
, and
\xi_{0}
maps each channel to
\varepsilon
.
A configuration
(\vec{s},\xi)
is
final
iff
\vec{s}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
is final for every
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
and
\xi
maps each channel to
\varepsilon
.
Runs and traces are defined in the expected way.
A run is
maximal
if either it is finite and ends in a final configuration, or it is infinite.
The language
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{T})
of the CLTS
\mathcal{T}
is defined as the set of maximal traces.
A configuration
(\vec{s},\xi)
is a
deadlock
if it is not final and has no outgoing transitions.
A CLTS is
deadlock-free
if no reachable configuration is a deadlock.

Observe that in a CLTS, send transitions are always enabled, whereas receive transitions are only enabled if the message exists at the head of its corresponding channel.
Communicating state machines
(
Brand and Zafiropulo, 1983
)
are a special case of CLTS where the LTS for each participant
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}
is a deterministic finite state machine.
Note that CLTS describe asynchronous communication with message channels of unbounded size. Thus, they differ from Zielonka’s
asynchronous automata

(
Zielonka, 1987
)
, which actually describe
synchronously communicating systems

(
Mukund, 2002
)
.
We refer the reader to
(
Diekert and Rozenberg, 1995
)
for further details.

Finally, we define protocol implementability.

Definition 3.3 (Protocol Implementability).

A protocol
\mathcal{S}
is
implementable
if there exists a CLTS
\{\!\!\{T_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\}\!\!\}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
such that the following two properties hold:

(i)

protocol fidelity
:
\mathcal{L}(\{\!\!\{T_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\}\!\!\}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}})=\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S})
, and

(ii)

deadlock freedom
:
\{\!\!\{T_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\}\!\!\}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
is deadlock-free.

We say that
\{\!\!\{T_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\}\!\!\}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
implements
\mathcal{S}
.

A notion of implementability that relaxes language equality to language inclusion has been studied as protocol refinement
(
Li et al., 2024
)
.
Alternatively, one can expand the set of protocol behaviors to include deadlocking behaviors, resulting in a notion of implementability that replaces language equality with prefix set equality, and waives the requirement of deadlock freedom.

3.2.
Symbolic protocols with dependent refinements

We now introduce our model for finitely representing infinite state protocols.
We refer to these representations simply as
symbolic protocols
.

Figure

7
shows the two-bidder protocol from
Fig.

2
expressed as a symbolic protocol.

q_{0}
q_{1}
q_{2}
q_{3}
q_{4}
q_{5}
q_{6}
q_{7}
q_{8}
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\hskip-4.52083pt\begin{array}[]{c}r_{y}=0\,\land\,r_{z}=0\\
\,\,\land\,r_{z_{1}}=0\,\land\,r_{z_{2}}=0\\
\end{array}\hskip-4.52083pt}}\right\}

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{1}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{S}}\!:\!y\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\hskip-4.52083pt\begin{array}[]{c}\mathsf{ISBN}(y)\\
\,\land\,r_{y}^{\prime}=y\end{array}\hskip-4.52083pt}}\right\}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{1}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{2}}}\!:\!y\{y=r_{y}\}

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{2}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{S}}\!:\!x\{x=\mathsf{quit}\}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{S}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{1}}}\!:\!x\{x=\mathsf{quit}\}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{2}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{1}}}\!:\!b_{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\hskip-4.52083pt\begin{array}[]{c}\,\,b_{2}>r_{z_{2}}\\
\,\land\,r^{\prime}_{z_{2}}=b_{2}\end{array}\hskip-4.52083pt}}\right\}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{1}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{S}}\!:\!x\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\hskip-4.52083pt\begin{array}[]{c}\,x=\mathsf{succ}\\
\,\land\,r_{z_{1}}+r_{z_{2}}\geq r_{z}\\
\end{array}\hskip-4.52083pt}}\right\}

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{1}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{2}}}\!:\!x\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\hskip-4.52083pt\begin{array}[]{c}x=\mathsf{cont}\\
\,\land\,r_{z_{1}}+r_{z_{2}}<r_{z}\end{array}\hskip-4.52083pt}}\right\}

Figure 7
.
The two-bidder protocol from
Fig.

2
as a symbolic protocol with registers
r_{z}
,
r_{y}
,
r_{z_{1}}
, and
r_{z_{2}}
.

The formal definition of this class of symbolic protocols is given below.
In this definition, we assume a fixed but unspecified first-order background theory of message values (e.g. linear integer arithmetic).
We assume standard syntax and semantics of first-order formulas and denote by
\mathcal{F}
the set of first-order formulas with free variables drawn from an infinite set
X
.
We assume that these variables are interpreted over the set of message values
\mathcal{V}
.
For a valuation
\rho\in X\to\mathcal{V}
and
\varphi\in\mathcal{F}(X)
, we write
\rho\models\phi
to indicate that
\varphi
evaluates to true under
\rho
in the underlying theory.

Definition 3.4 (Symbolic protocol).

A symbolic protocol is a tuple

\mathbb{S}=(S,R,\Delta,s_{0},\rho_{0},F)

where

•

S
is a finite set of control states,

•

R
is a finite set of register variables,

•

\Delta\subseteq S\times\mathcal{P}\times X\times\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{F}\times S
is a finite set that consists of symbolic transitions of the form
s\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}:x\{\varphi\}}s^{\prime}
where the formula
\varphi
with free variables
R\uplus R^{\prime}\uplus\{x\}
expresses a transition constraint that relates the old and new register values (
R
and
R^{\prime}
), and the sent value
x
,

•

s_{0}\in S
is the initial control state,

•

\rho_{0}\colon R\rightarrow\mathcal{V}
is the initial register assignment, and

•

F\subseteq S
is a set of final states.

To streamline our definition, we choose not to separate
register update expressions from predicates describing the communication.
Rather, we specify everything together in a single formula
\varphi
, that can only talk about the current value being communicated, and the register values in the pre- and post-state.
Thus,
\varphi
can describe values that are communicated between participants, in addition to register assignments and updates.
For example,
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}:x\{even(x)\land r_{1}^{\prime}=r_{1}+2\land r_{2}^{\prime}=x\}
describes
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
sending
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}
an even number
x
, incrementing the value of register
r_{1}
by 2, and storing the value of
x
in register
r_{2}
.
We formally specify the two-bidder protocol from
Fig.

2
as a symbolic protocol in
Fig.

7
for demonstration purposes; note that the transition predicate
ISBN(y)
from
q_{1}
to
q_{2}
is replaced with an equality.
For readability and conciseness, we employ the following conventions from now on.
We treat communication variables as registers that are automatically assigned the communicated value, e.g.
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{S}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{1}}}\!:\!z\{z>0\}

should be understood as

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{S}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{1}}}\!:\!x\{x>0\land z^{\prime}=x\}
for some fresh
x
.
Furthermore, if the communicated value is a constant
c
and there is no need to store this value, we inline it and write

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{S}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{2}}}\!:\!\mathsf{succ}\{\top\}

instead of

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{S}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{2}}}\!:\!x\{x=\mathsf{succ}\}
.
We may omit the condition
\top
,
turning

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{S}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{2}}}\!:\!\mathsf{succ}\{\top\}

into

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{S}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{B_{2}}}\!:\!\mathsf{succ}
.

Symbolic protocols are specification-wise similar to symbolic register automata
(
D’Antoni et al., 2019
)
, but allow more general patterns of register manipulation and do not a priori require formulas to come from an effective Boolean algebra.
Symbolic protocols can be seen as a finite description of an infinite-state LTS, whose concrete states consist of a control state along with an assignment for the register variables
R
. Transitions are concrete communication events that optionally modify register values.
We formally define the concretization of a symbolic protocol below.

Definition 3.5 (Concretization of symbolic protocols).

For a symbolic protocol
\mathbb{S}=(S,R,\Delta,s_{0},\rho_{0},F)
, let
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{S}}
denote its concrete protocol.
The set of states of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{S}}
is
S\times(R\to\mathcal{V})
.

Transitions in
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{S}}
are defined as follows:

\frac{s_{1}\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}:x\{\varphi\}}s_{2}\in\Delta\quad\rho_{1}\rho_{2}^{\prime}[x\mapsto v]\models\varphi}{(s_{1},\rho_{1})\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}:v}(s_{2},\rho_{2})}

Intuitively, the rule says that a symbolic transition from
s_{1}
to
s_{2}
can be instantiated to one from
(s_{1},\rho_{1})
to
(s_{2},\rho_{2})
on value
v
when
v
together with the register assignments in the pre- and post-state satisfy the transition constraint
\varphi
. Here, we use juxtaposition
\rho_{1}\rho_{2}^{\prime}
of register assignments to express their disjoint union. The assignment
\rho_{2}^{\prime}
is obtained from
\rho_{2}
by replacing registers
r
in the domain with their primed version in
R^{\prime}
.
The initial state is defined as
(s_{0},\rho_{0})
.
A state
(s,\rho)
in
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{S}}
is final when
s\in F
.

Thus, the concrete protocol
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{S}}
is a protocol over the alphabet
\Gamma_{\negthinspace\mathit{sync}}
.
The language of a symbolic protocol
\mathbb{S}
is defined as the language of its concretization
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{S}}
.
Consequently, a symbolic protocol is implementable if its concretization is implementable.

4.
Characterizing Protocol Implementability

We motivate our precise characterization of protocol implementability through examples of non-implementable protocols, and show that seemingly disparate sources of non-implementability share a unified semantic explanation.
Recall the protocol
\mathbb{S}_{1}
from
Section

2
with a receiver violation, depicted in
Fig.

5
.
The infinite-state LTS
\mathbb{S}_{1}
contains the two concrete run prefixes depicted in

Fig.

8
, where the values of
x,y
are
2,3
and
1,3
respectively.

(a)

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!:\!2
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\!:\!3
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{b}}
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\mathsf{o}}
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\mathsf{o}}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{b}}

(b)

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!:\!1
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\!:\!3
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathsf{m}}
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\mathsf{o}}
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\mathsf{o}}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathsf{m}}

Figure 8
.

Two concrete runs of
\mathbb{S}_{1}
(
Fig.

5
):
(a) with
x=2
and
y=3
and
(b) with
x=1
and
y=3
.

Inspecting
\mathbb{S}_{1}
’s specification reveals that the protocol expects
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
to receive messages from
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}
in a different order depending on the branch that
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}
chooses to follow.
However, this expectation is unreasonable in a distributed setting.
Between the two concrete runs,

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
’s partial view of the protocol’s behavior is the same:
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
receives a value 3 from
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
, yet
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
is expected to receive in
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}
order in one run, and receive in
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
order in the other.

Recall the protocol
\mathbb{S}_{2}
from
Section

2
with a sender violation, depicted in

Fig.

6
.

q_{1}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{b}}
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!:\!4
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\mathsf{o}}
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathsf{m}}

Figure 9
.

A concrete run of
\mathbb{S}_{2}
(
Fig.

6
) with
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}
choosing the top branch.

q_{2}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathsf{m}}
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!:\!4
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{b}}

Figure 10
.

A concrete run of
\mathbb{S}_{2}
(
Fig.

6
) with

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}
choosing the bottom branch and
x_{2}=4
.

Again inspecting
\mathbb{S}_{2}
’s specification, the branching structure imposes the expectation that on the top branch,
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
should send
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}
an
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\mathsf{o}}
message, whereas on the bottom branch,
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
should immediately terminate.
The two concrete runs in
Fig.

10
and
Fig.

10
again demonstrate that this expectation is unreasonable:
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
receives the value 3 from
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}
in both runs, but in one run is expected to send a message, whereas in the other is expected to terminate.

The non-implementability in the examples above can be attributed to insufficient local information about protocol control flow.
This source of non-implementability is inherent to the expressive power of branching choice in protocol specifications, and is present even in finite protocols with more restricted choice constructs.
While most existing works soundly detect insufficient local information through conservative projection algorithms
(
Honda et al., 2008
;
Coppo et al., 2015
;
Toninho and Yoshida, 2017
;
Scalas et al., 2017
)
,
Li et al. (2023a)
give a complete characterization.
To check implementability,
they
first obtain a candidate implementation by restricting the global protocol onto each participant’s alphabet, and then determinizing the resulting finite state automaton.
Then,
they
check sound and complete conditions directly on the states of the candidate implementation.

Our first observation towards a precise characterization is that implementability can be checked directly on the global protocol specification, without synthesizing a candidate implementation upfront.
This is especially important in the general case, when synthesizing a candidate implementation is itself challenging and not always possible.
Our analysis of the protocols above shows that non-implementability can be blamed solely on the existence of certain states in the concrete LTS represented by the global protocol.
In fact, we show in §
5
that the implementability check for global types by
Li et al. (2023a)
can be made more efficient by forgoing the synthesis step.

Let us now turn our attention to a different source of non-implementability that is unique to the setting of dependent data refinements.
Consider the following pair of symbolic protocols
\mathbb{S}_{3}
and
\mathbb{S}_{3}^{\prime}
, depicted in
Fig.

12
and
Fig.

12
.

{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!:\!x\{\top\}
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\!:\!y\{y>x\}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!:\!z\{z>x\}

Figure 11
.
\mathbb{S}_{3}
: A non-implementable protocol with

dependent refinements.

{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!:\!x\{\top\}
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\!:\!y\{y=x\}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!:\!z\{z>x\}

Figure 12
.
\mathbb{S}_{3}^{\prime}
: An implementable protocol with

dependent refinements.

Non-implementability is again caused by insufficient local information, but this time with respect to message data rather than control flow: in fact, no branching choice appears in this pair of simple protocols.
The problem instead arises in the fact that in both
\mathbb{S}_{3}
and
\mathbb{S}_{3}^{\prime}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
does not know the value of
x
.
While an implementation for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
could produce a subset of
\mathbb{S}_{3}
’s behaviors (e.g. by sending
z
such that
z>y
), or produce a superset of
\mathbb{S}_{3}
’s behaviors (e.g. by sending all values for
z
), no implementation can produce exactly the specified behaviors, as required by protocol fidelity.

Zhou et al. (2020)
address partial information of protocol variables by syntactically classifying whether a variable is known or unknown to a participant, and annotating the variables accordingly in the typing context: a variable is known to its sender and receiver, and unknown to all other participants.
However, this syntactic analysis is itself insufficient, as demonstrated by these examples: both protocols yield the same classification of variables per participant, yet one is implementable and the other is not.

We instead turn to concrete runs of
\mathbb{S}_{3}
to find the source of non-implementability.
Let us consider the concrete runs of
\mathbb{S}_{3}

depicted in

Fig.

13

, where the values of
x,y
are
2,4
and
3,4
respectively.

(a)

{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!:\!2
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\!:\!4
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!:\!3

(b)

{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!:\!3
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\!:\!4
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!:\!4

Figure 13
.

Two concrete runs of
\mathbb{S}_{3}
(
Fig.

12
):
(a) with
x=2,y=4,z=3
and
(b) with
x=3,y=4,z=4
.

In this pair of runs,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
observes the same behaviors, namely receiving the value 4 from
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}
.
While
\mathbb{S}_{3}
also permits
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
to send 4 to
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
in the first run, sending 3 to
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
in the second run constitutes a violation to the refinement predicate
z>x
, i.e.
3>3
is false.
Again, this presents a problem because the two run prefixes are indistinguishable to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
.
Observe that in this example, non-implementability can again be blamed solely on the existence of states in the global protocol.

We formalize a participant’s local information about the protocol using two variations on the standard notion of reachability.
Let
\mathcal{S}=(S,\Gamma_{\negthinspace\mathit{sync}},T,s_{0},F)
be a protocol and let
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}
be a participant.
The standard notion defines
s^{\prime}
as reachable from
s
in
\mathcal{S}
on
w\in\Gamma_{\negthinspace\mathit{sync}}^{*}
, denoted

s\xrightarrow{w}\mathrel{\vphantom{\to}{}^{*}}s^{\prime}
, when there exists a sequence of transitions
s_{1}\xrightarrow{l_{1}}s_{2}\ldots s_{n-1}\xrightarrow{l_{n-1}}s_{n}
, such that
s_{1}=s
,
s_{n}=s^{\prime}
,
l_{1}\ldots l_{n-1}=w
and for each
1\leq i<n
, it holds that
s_{i}\xrightarrow{l_{i}}s_{i+1}\in T
.
We first define a notion of reachability that restricts the transitions to only the actions observable by a single participant.

Participant-based Reachability.

We say that
s\in S
is
reachable for
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
on
u\in\Gamma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}^{*}
when there exists
w\in\Gamma_{\negthinspace\mathit{sync}}^{*}
such that

s_{0}\xrightarrow{w}\mathrel{\vphantom{\to}{}^{*}}s\in T
and
w{\Downarrow}_{\Gamma_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}=u
,
which we denote
s_{0}\xRightarrow[{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}]{u}\mathrel{\vphantom{\to}{}^{*}}s
.
We characterize
simultaneously reachable
pairs of states for each participant using the notion of participant-based reachability.

Simultaneous Reachability.

We say that
s_{1},s_{2}\in S
are simultaneously reachable for participant
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
on
u\in\Gamma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}^{*}
, denoted

s_{0}\xRightarrow[{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}]{u}\mathrel{\vphantom{\to}{}^{*}}s_{1},s_{2}
,
if there exist
w_{1},w_{2}\in\Gamma_{\negthinspace\mathit{sync}}^{*}
such that
s_{0}\xrightarrow{w_{1}}\mathrel{\vphantom{\to}{}^{*}}s_{1}\in T,s_{0}\xrightarrow{w_{2}}\mathrel{\vphantom{\to}{}^{*}}s_{2}\in T
and
w_{1}{\Downarrow}_{\Gamma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}=w_{2}{\Downarrow}_{\Gamma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}=u
.
Simultaneous reachability captures the notion of
locally indistinguishable
states: to a participant, two states are locally indistinguishable if they are simultaneously reachable.
Send Coherence requires that any message that can be sent from a state can also be sent from all other states that are locally indistinguishable to the sender.

Definition 4.1 (Send Coherence).

A protocol

\mathcal{S}=(S,\Gamma_{\negthinspace\mathit{sync}},T,s_{0},F)

satisfies Send Coherence (SC) when for every
s_{1}\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}:m}s_{2}\in T,s_{1}^{\prime}\in S
:

(\exists u\in\Gamma_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}^{*}.~s_{0}\xRightarrow[{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}]{u}\mathrel{\vphantom{\to}{}^{*}}s_{1},s_{1}^{\prime})\implies(\exists s_{2}^{\prime}\in S.~s_{1}^{\prime}\xRightarrow[{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}]{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}:m}\mathrel{\vphantom{\to}{}^{*}}s_{2}^{\prime})\kern 5.0pt.

Receive Coherence, on the other hand, requires that no message which can be received from a state can be received from any other state that is locally indistinguishable to the receiver.

Definition 4.2 (Receive Coherence).

A protocol

\mathcal{S}=(S,\Gamma_{\negthinspace\mathit{sync}},T,s_{0},F)

satisfies Receive Coherence (RC) when for every
s_{1}\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}:m}s_{2},s_{1}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}:m}s_{2}^{\prime}\in T
:

({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\neq{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\,\land\,\exists u\in\Gamma_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}^{*}.~s_{0}\xRightarrow[{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}]{u}\mathrel{\vphantom{\to}{}^{*}}s_{1},s_{1}^{\prime})\!\implies\!\forall w\in\operatorname{pref}(\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}_{s_{2}^{\prime}})).~w{\Downarrow}_{Σ_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}\!\neq\!\varepsilon\,\lor\,\mathcal{V}(w{\Downarrow}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}!\_})\!\neq\!\mathcal{V}(w{\Downarrow}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\triangleleft{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}?\_})\cdot m)\;.

No Mixed Choice requires that roles cannot equivocate between sending and receiving in two locally indistinguishable states.

Definition 4.3 (No Mixed Choice).

A protocol

\mathcal{S}=(S,\Gamma_{\negthinspace\mathit{sync}},T,s_{0},F)

satisfies No Mixed Choice (NMC) when for every
s_{1}\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}:m}s_{2},s_{1}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}:m}s_{2}^{\prime}\in T
:

(\exists u\in\Gamma_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}^{*}.~s_{0}\xRightarrow[{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}]{u}\mathrel{\vphantom{\to}{}^{*}}s_{1},s_{1}^{\prime})\implies\bot
.

Our semantic characterization of protocol implementability is the conjunction of the above three conditions.
In contrast to the syntactic analysis in
(
Zhou et al., 2020
)
, our semantic approach is sound and complete.
In contrast to the sound and complete approach in
(
Li et al., 2023a
)
, our implementability conditions do not rely on synthesizing an implementation upfront.

Definition 4.4 (Coherence Conditions).

A protocol
satisfies Coherence Conditions (CC) when it satisfies Send Coherence, Receive Coherence and No Mixed Choice.

The preciseness of
CC
is stated as follows.

Theorem 4.5.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a protocol.
Then,
\mathcal{S}
is implementable if and only if it satisfies CC.

In the next two sections, we illustrate the key steps for proving
Theorem

4.5
. We refer the reader to
Appendix

A
for the complete proofs.

4.1.
Soundness

Soundness requires us to show that if a protocol satisfies
CC
, then it is implementable.
We begin by echoing the observation made in several prior works
(
Alur et al., 2003
;
Stutz, 2023
;
Li et al., 2023a
)
that for any global protocol, there exists a
canonical
implementation consisting of one local implementation per participant.
We formally define what it means for an implementation to be canonical in our setting below.

Definition 4.6 (Canonical implementations).

We say a CLTS
\{\!\!\{T_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\}\!\!\}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
is a
canonical implementation
for a protocol
\mathcal{S}=(S,\Gamma_{\negthinspace\mathit{sync}},T,s_{0},F)

if for every
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}
,
T_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
satisfies:

(i)

\forall w\in Σ_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}^{*}.~w\in\mathcal{L}(T_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}})\Leftrightarrow w\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}){\Downarrow}_{Σ_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
, and

(ii)

\operatorname{pref}(\mathcal{L}(T_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}))=\operatorname{pref}(\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}){\Downarrow}_{Σ_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
.

We first prove that following fact about canonical implementations of protocols satisfying NMC, which states that the canonical implementations themselves do not exhibit mixed choice.

Lemma 4.7 (No Mixed Choice).

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a protocol satisfying NMC (
Definition

4.3
) and let
\{\!\!\{T_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\}\!\!\}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
be a canonical implementation for
\mathcal{S}
.
Let
wx_{1},wx_{2}\in\operatorname{pref}(\mathcal{L}(T_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}))
with
x_{1}\neq x_{2}
for some
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}
.
Then,
x_{1}\in Σ_{!}
iff
x_{2}\in Σ_{!}
.

We choose the canonical implementation as our existential witness to show that any protocol satisfying
CC
is implementable.
By the definition of implementability (
Definition

3.3
), soundness amounts to showing the following three conditions:

(a)

\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S})\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\{\!\!\{T_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\}\!\!\}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}})
,

(b)

\mathcal{L}(\{\!\!\{T_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\}\!\!\}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}})\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S})
, and

(c)

\{\!\!\{T_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\}\!\!\}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
is deadlock-free.

Condition (a) states that any canonical implementation recognizes at least the global protocol behaviors. This fact can be shown for any LTS and canonical CLTS, and does not rely on assumptions about determinism or sender-drivenness, nor assumptions about the LTS satisfying
CC
.

Lemma 4.8 (Canonical implementation language contains protocol language).

Let
\mathcal{S}
be an LTS and let
\{\!\!\{T_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\}\!\!\}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
be a canonical implementation for
\mathcal{S}
.
Then,
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S})\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\{\!\!\{T_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\}\!\!\}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}})
.

Condition (b), on the other hand, states that any behavior recognized by the canonical implementation is a global protocol behavior, in other words, that the canonical CLTS does not add behaviors. This is only true for protocols that satisfy
CC
.

Furthermore, the acceptance condition for infinite words in
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S})
differs from that in
\{\!\!\{T_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\}\!\!\}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
: the latter accepts all infinite traces, whereas the former requires to show that an infinite word
w
satisfies
w\preceq_{\sim}^{\omega}w^{\prime}
for some other infinite word
w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S})
.
Therefore, showing prefix set inclusion is not sufficient, and we must reason about the finite and infinite case separately.

Lemma 4.9 (Global protocol language contains canonical implementation language).

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a protocol satisfying
CC
and let
\{\!\!\{T_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\}\!\!\}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
be a canonical implementation for
\mathcal{S}
such that for all
w\in Σ_{\mathit{async}}^{*}
, if
w
is a trace of
\{\!\!\{T_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\}\!\!\}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
, then
I(w)\neq\emptyset
.

Then,
\mathcal{L}(\{\!\!\{T_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\}\!\!\}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}})\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S})
.

Towards these ends, we adapt the key intermediate lemma from
(
Li et al., 2023a
)
to our setting, and show the inductive invariant that every trace in the canonical implementation of a protocol satisfying
CC
satisfies
intersection set non-emptiness
.
Note that although our intermediate lemma statements are similar to those in
(
Li et al., 2023a
)
in structure,
(
Li et al., 2023a
)
reasons about a particular implementation, namely the subset construction obtained from the global type, whereas our proofs reason about any canonical implementation of a global protocol that satisfies
CC
.
As a result, the proof arguments differ significantly.

We adapt the relevant definitions to our setting below.

Definition 4.10 (LTS intersection sets).

Let
\mathcal{S}

be an LTS.
Let
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
be a participant and

w\in Σ_{\mathit{async}}^{*}
be a word.
We define the set of possible runs
\operatorname{R}^{\mathcal{S}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}(w)

as all maximal runs of
\mathcal{S}
that are consistent with
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
’s local view of
w
:

\operatorname{R}^{\mathcal{S}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}(w)\coloneq\{\rho\text{ is a maximal run of }\mathcal{S}\mid w{\Downarrow}_{Σ_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\leq\texttt{split}(\texttt{trace}(\rho)){\Downarrow}_{Σ_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\kern 5.0pt.

We denote the intersection of the possible run sets for all participants as

I^{\mathcal{S}}(w)\coloneq\bigcap_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}}\operatorname{R}^{\mathcal{S}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}(w)
.

Definition 4.11 (Unique splitting of a possible run).

Let
\mathcal{S}
be an LTS,
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
a participant, and
w\in Σ_{\mathit{async}}^{*}
a word.
Let
\rho
be a run in
\operatorname{R}^{\mathcal{S}}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}(w)
.
We define the longest prefix of
\rho
matching
w
:

\alpha^{\prime}\coloneq\max\{\rho^{\prime}\mid\rho^{\prime}\leq\rho~\wedge~\texttt{split}(\texttt{trace}(\rho^{\prime})){\Downarrow}_{Σ_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\leq w{\Downarrow}_{Σ_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\kern 5.0pt.

If
\alpha^{\prime}\neq\rho
, we can split
\rho
into

\rho=\alpha\cdot s\xrightarrow{l}s^{\prime}\cdot\beta

where
\alpha^{\prime}=\alpha\cdot s
.
, which we call the unique splitting of
\rho
for
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
matching
w
.
Uniqueness follows from the maximality of
\alpha^{\prime}
.

For example, the unique splitting of

\rho=s_{1}\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathsf{m}}}s_{2}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{b}}_{1}}s_{3}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{b}}_{2}}s_{4}\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}:{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\mathsf{o}}}s_{5}

for
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
matching

w={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{b}}_{1}.\,{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}!{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathsf{m}}
is

\alpha\cdot s_{3}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{b}}_{2}}s_{4}\cdot\beta
,
where
\alpha=s_{1}\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathsf{m}}}s_{2}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{b}}_{1}}s_{3}
and
\beta=s_{4}\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}:{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\mathsf{o}}}s_{5}
.
Our intersection non-emptiness inductive invariant is stated below.
The proof proceeds by induction on the length of a prefix
w
of the canonical implementation, and case splits based on whether
w
is extended by a send or receive action.

Lemma

4.14
and
Lemma

4.13
provide a characterization for each case respectively.

Lemma 4.12 (Intersection set non-emptiness).

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a protocol satisfying
CC
, and let
\{\!\!\{T_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\}\!\!\}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
be a canonical implementation for
\mathcal{S}
.
Then, for every trace
w\in Σ_{\mathit{async}}^{*}
of
\{\!\!\{T_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\}\!\!\}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
, it holds that
