I(w)\neq\emptyset
.

Lemma 4.13 (Receive events do not shrink intersection sets).

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a protocol satisfying
CC
, and let
\{\!\!\{T_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\}\!\!\}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
be a canonical implementation for
\mathcal{S}
.
Let
wx
be a trace of
\{\!\!\{T_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\}\!\!\}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
such that
x\in Σ_{?}
.
Then,
I(w)=I(wx)
.

Lemma 4.14 (Send events preserve run prefixes).

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a protocol satisfying
CC
and
\{\!\!\{T_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\}\!\!\}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
be a canonical implementation for
\mathcal{S}
.
Let
wx
be a trace of

\{\!\!\{T_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\}\!\!\}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
such that
x\in Σ_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}},!}

for some
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}
.
Let
\rho
be a run in
I(w)
, and
\alpha\cdot s_{pre}\xrightarrow{l}s_{post}\cdot\beta
be the unique splitting of
\rho
for
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
with respect to
w
.
Then, there exists a run
\rho^{\prime}
in
I(wx)
such that
\alpha\cdot s_{pre}\leq\rho^{\prime}
.

Finally, we show that protocols that satisfy
CC
and intersection set non-emptiness have deadlock-free canonical implementations. The proof follows immediately from the following lemma and the fact that CLTS are deterministic, and is thus omitted.

Lemma 4.15 (Channel compliance and intersection set non-emptiness implies prefix).

Let
\mathcal{S}=(S,\Gamma_{\negthinspace\mathit{sync}},T,s_{0},F)
be a protocol and let
w\in Σ_{\mathit{async}}^{*}
be a word such that

(i)
w
is channel-compliant, and
(ii)
I(w)\neq\emptyset
.
Then,
w\in\operatorname{pref}(\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}))
.

Lemma 4.16 (Canonical implementation deadlock freedom).

Let
\mathcal{S}=(S,\Gamma_{\negthinspace\mathit{sync}},T,s_{0},F)
be a protocol satisfying
CC
and let
\{\!\!\{T_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\}\!\!\}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
be a canonical implementation for
\mathcal{S}
such that for all
w\in Σ_{\mathit{async}}^{*}
, if
w
is a trace of
\{\!\!\{T_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\}\!\!\}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
, then
I(w)\neq\emptyset
.
Then,
\{\!\!\{T_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\}\!\!\}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
is deadlock-free.

Soundness thus follows from the three conditions above.

Lemma 4.17 (Soundness of
CC
).

Let
\mathcal{S}

be a protocol.
If
\mathcal{S}
satisfies
CC
, then
\mathcal{S}
is implementable.

4.2.
Completeness

Completeness requires us to show that if a protocol is implementable, then it satisfies
CC
.
We prove completeness by modus tollens, and assume that a protocol
\mathcal{S}
does not satisfy
CC
.
We negate SC, RC and NMC in turn: from the negation of SC we obtain a simultaneously reachable pair of states in
\mathcal{S}
such that a send event that is enabled in one is never enabled from the other. From the negation of RC there exists a simultaneously reachable pair of states in
\mathcal{S}
such that a receive event that is enabled in one is also enabled in the other. From the negation of NMC we obtain a simultaneously reachable pair of transitions where a participant is the sender in one and the receiver in the other.
We assume an arbitrary CLTS that implements
\mathcal{S}
, and use these witnesses to show that this CLTS must recognize a trace that is not a prefix in
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S})
, thereby either violating protocol fidelity or deadlock freedom.

Lemma 4.18 (Completeness).

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a protocol.
If
\mathcal{S}
is implementable, then
\mathcal{S}
satisfies
CC
.

An immediate consequence of the soundness and completeness of
CC
is the following fact about the special case of binary protocols, when
\lvert\mathcal{P}\rvert=2
:

Lemma 4.19.

Every binary protocol is implementable.

In the binary case, participant-based reachability is equivalent to standard reachability, because both participants are involved in every synchronous communication. Because protocols are deterministic, there exist no two distinct states in a binary protocol that are simultaneously reachable for either participant, and thus
CC
holds vacuously.

4.3.
Synthesis

When proving soundness, we chose the canonical implementation as our witness to implementability.
In other words, if a protocol satisfies
CC
, then the canonical implementation implements it.
When proving completeness, we showed that
any
implementation would cause a violation to protocol fidelity or deadlock-freedom.
In other words, if a protocol violates
CC
, then no implementation exists.
Having established that
CC
precisely characterizes implementable protocols, we combine these observations to yield the following corollary:

Corollary 4.20 (Canonical implementation is all you need).

A protocol is implementable if and only if the canonical implementation implements it.

For an implementable protocol, this fact serves as a criterion for synthesizing implementations: any implementation that is canonical will suffice.
For the general class of protocols, synthesis is undecidable.
However, for many expressive fragments of protocols that still feature infinite data, e.g. corresponding to symbolic finite automata
(
D’Antoni and Veanes, 2017
;
Shen et al., 2023
)
and certain classes of timed and register automata
(
Bertrand et al., 2015
;
Clemente et al., 2022
)
, one can simply use off-the-shelf determinization algorithms to compute canonical implementations
(
Veanes et al., 2010
;
Veanes and Bjørner, 2012
;
Bertrand et al., 2018
)
.

5.
Checking Implementability

Having established that
CC
is precise for protocol implementability, we next present sound and relatively complete algorithms to check
CC
for several protocol classes. We start with the most general case of symbolic protocols before considering decidable classes of finite-state protocols.

5.1.
Symbolic Protocols

In the remainder of the section, we fix a symbolic protocol
\mathbb{S}=(S,R,\Delta,s_{0},\rho_{0},F)
.
We assume that the concretization of
\mathbb{S}
is a GCLTS (
Definition

3.1
).
Additionally, we define two copies of the symbolic protocol, denoted
\mathbb{S}_{1}
and
\mathbb{S}_{2}
that we will use in describing our symbolic implementability check.
Each copy
\mathbb{S}_{i}=(R_{i},S,\Delta_{i},\rho_{i},s_{0},F)
with
i\in\{1,2\}
is obtained from
\mathbb{S}
by renaming each register
r
to a fresh register
r_{i}
, each unique communication variable
x
to
x_{i}
, and substituting the new register and communication variables into the transition constraints and initial register assignment accordingly; the control states remain the same.

Because symbolic protocols describe concrete protocols with infinitely many states and transitions, implementability cannot be checked explicitly using our
CC
characterization for protocols, i.e. by iterating over all states and transitions.
Instead, we present symbolic conditions that are valid on the symbolic protocol if and only if its concrete protocol is implementable.

Theorem 5.1 (Symbolic Implementability).

\mathbb{S}
is implementable if and only if it satisfies Symbolic Send Coherence, Symbolic Receive Coherence, and Symbolic No Mixed Choice.

We now present these symbolic conditions, starting with Symbolic Send Coherence.

Send Coherence first requires us to characterize pairs of states in a protocol that are simultaneously reachable for each participant on some prefix in its local language.
In the symbolic setting, this amounts to the following: given a participant and a pair of control states
(s_{1},s_{2})
in the symbolic protocol, characterize the register assignments for pairs of concrete states
(s_{1},\rho_{1})
,
(s_{2},\rho_{2})
that are in the respective control states.
The predicate
\mathsf{prodreach}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}(s_{1},\boldsymbol{r_{1}},s_{2},\boldsymbol{r_{2}})
describes this for each
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}
where
\boldsymbol{r_{i}}
are vectors of the registers in
R_{i}
obtained by ordering them according to some fixed total order. We define this predicate as a least fixpoint as follows.

Definition 5.2 (Simultaneous reachability in product symbolic protocol).

Let
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}
be a participant and let
s_{1},s_{1}^{\prime},s_{2},s_{2}^{\prime}\in S
.
Then,

\displaystyle\mathsf{prodreach}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}(s_{1}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{r_{1}^{\prime}},s_{2}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{r_{2}^{\prime}})\coloneq_{\mu}(\,s_{1}^{\prime}=s_{0}\land s_{2}^{\prime}=s_{0}\land\boldsymbol{r_{1}^{\prime}}=\rho_{0}\land\boldsymbol{r_{2}^{\prime}}=\rho_{0}\,)

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\lor(\bigvee_{\begin{subarray}{c}(s_{1},\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\to{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}:x_{1}\{\varphi_{1}\},\,s_{1}^{\prime})\in\Delta_{1}\\
(s_{2},\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\to{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}:x_{2}\{\varphi_{2}\},\,s_{2}^{\prime})\in\Delta_{2}\\
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\lor{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}\end{subarray}}\hskip-7.74997pt\exists x_{1}x_{2}\boldsymbol{r_{1}}\boldsymbol{r_{2}}.\,\mathsf{prodreach}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}(s_{1},\boldsymbol{r_{1}},s_{2},\boldsymbol{r_{2}})\land\varphi_{1}\land\varphi_{2}\land x_{1}=x_{2}\,)

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\lor(\bigvee_{\begin{subarray}{c}(s_{1},\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\to{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}:x_{1}\{\varphi_{1}\},\,s_{1}^{\prime})\in\Delta_{1}\,\land\,{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\land{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}\end{subarray}}\hskip-15.49994pt\exists x_{1}\boldsymbol{r_{1}}.\,\mathsf{prodreach}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}(s_{1},\boldsymbol{r_{1}},s_{2}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{r_{2}^{\prime}})\land\varphi_{1}\,)

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\lor(\bigvee_{\begin{subarray}{c}(s_{2},\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\to{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}:x_{2}\{\varphi_{2}\},\,s_{2}^{\prime})\in\Delta_{2}\,\land\,{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\land{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}\end{subarray}}\hskip-15.49994pt\exists x_{2}\boldsymbol{r_{2}}.\,\mathsf{prodreach}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}(s_{1}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{r_{1}^{\prime}},s_{2},\boldsymbol{r_{2}})\land\varphi_{2}\,)\kern 4.62497pt.

The second top-level disjunct in the definition after the base case handles the cases where
\mathbb{S}_{1}
and
\mathbb{S}_{2}
synchronize on a common action involving
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
. The remaining two disjuncts correspond to the cases where either
\mathbb{S}_{1}
or
\mathbb{S}_{2}
follows an
\varepsilon
transition.
Given a pair of simultaneously reachable states
(s_{1},\rho_{1})
,
(s_{2},\rho_{2})
in
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
, Send Coherence now checks whether all values
x_{1}
that can be sent to some
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}
in
(s_{1},\rho_{1})
can also be sent from
(s_{2},\rho_{2})
, modulo following
\varepsilon
transitions to reach the actual state where
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
can send to
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}
. We thus need to express
\varepsilon
-reachability. We formalize the dual: the predicate
\mathsf{unreach}^{\varepsilon}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}(s_{2},\boldsymbol{r_{2}},x_{1})
expresses that
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}

cannot
reach any state where it may send
x_{1}
to
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}
, by following
\varepsilon
transitions from symbolic state
(s_{2},\boldsymbol{r_{2}})
. This is formulated as a greatest fixpoint as follows:

Definition 5.3 (
\varepsilon
-unreachability of
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!
sending
x
to
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}
).

For
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\in\mathcal{P}
and
s\in S
, let

\displaystyle\mathsf{unreach}^{\varepsilon}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}(s,\boldsymbol{r},x)\coloneq_{\nu}

\displaystyle(\bigwedge_{\begin{subarray}{c}(s,\,{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}:y\{\varphi\},\,s^{\prime})\in\Delta\end{subarray}}\hskip-11.62495pt\neg\varphi[x/y]\,)

\displaystyle\land(\bigwedge_{\begin{subarray}{c}(s,\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\to{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{t}}}:y\{\varphi\},\,s^{\prime})\in\Delta\\
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\land{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{t}}}\end{subarray}}\hskip-11.62495pt\forall y~\boldsymbol{r^{\prime}}.\,\varphi\Rightarrow\mathsf{unreach}^{\varepsilon}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}(s^{\prime},\boldsymbol{r^{\prime}},x)\,)\kern 4.62497pt.

The first conjunct checks that whenever
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
reaches a state with an outgoing send transition to
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}
, it cannot send the value
x
because the transition constraint
\varphi
is not satisfied. The second conjunct checks that every outgoing
\varepsilon
transition is either disabled (
\neg\varphi
holds) or following the transition does not reach an appropriate send state.

We combine the auxiliary predicates into our Symbolic Send Coherence condition.

Definition 5.4 (Symbolic Send Coherence).

A symbolic protocol
\mathbb{S}
satisfies Symbolic Send Coherence when for each transition
s_{1}\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}:x_{1}\{\varphi_{1}\}}s_{1}^{\prime}\in\Delta_{1}
and state
s_{2}\in S
, the following is valid:

\mathsf{prodreach}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}(s_{1},\boldsymbol{r_{1}},s_{2},\boldsymbol{r_{2}})~\land~\varphi_{1}\land\mathsf{unreach}^{\varepsilon}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}(s_{2},\boldsymbol{r_{2}},x_{1})\implies\bot\kern 4.62497pt.

A keen reader may have noticed that because the symbolic characterization of Send Coherence involves a greatest fixpoint, it is a liveness property. Thus, proving Send Coherence, in general, involves a termination argument. To see this, consider the two protocols shown in
Figs.

15
and
15
. Consider the pair of states
(q_{1},[c\mapsto 0])
and
(q_{3},[c\mapsto 0])
which are simultaneously reachable for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
in both protocols. The send transition for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
enabled in
q_{1}
needs to be matched with a corresponding send transition in an
\varepsilon
-reachable state from
q_{3}
. The only candidate states for this match in both protocols are those at control state
q_{4}
. These states are reachable from
q_{3}
if and only if the loop in
q_{3}
terminates, which it does in
Fig.

15
but not in
Fig.

15
.

q_{0}
q_{1}
q_{2}
q_{3}
q_{4}
q_{5}
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!:\!b\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\hskip-4.52083pt\begin{array}[]{c}b=0\\
\land\,c^{\prime}=0\end{array}\hskip-4.52083pt}}\right\}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!:\!x\{\top\}
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!:\!b\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\hskip-4.52083pt\begin{array}[]{c}b>0\\
\land\,c^{\prime}=0\end{array}\hskip-4.52083pt}}\right\}
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!:\!c\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\hskip-4.52083pt\begin{array}[]{c}c<b\\
\land\,c^{\prime}=c+1\end{array}\hskip-4.52083pt}}\right\}

{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!:\!\mathsf{exit}\{c\geq b\}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!:\!x\{\top\}

Figure 14
.

Example where states
q_{1}
and
q_{3}
satisfy

Send Coherence for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
.

q_{0}
q_{1}
q_{2}
q_{3}
q_{4}
q_{5}
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!:\!b\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\hskip-4.52083pt\begin{array}[]{c}b=0\\
\land\,c^{\prime}=0\end{array}\hskip-4.52083pt}}\right\}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!:\!x\{\top\}
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!:\!b\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\hskip-4.52083pt\begin{array}[]{c}b>0\\
\land\,c^{\prime}=0\end{array}\hskip-4.52083pt}}\right\}
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!:\!c\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\hskip-4.52083pt\begin{array}[]{c}c\geq 0\\
\land\,c^{\prime}=c+1\end{array}\hskip-4.52083pt}}\right\}

{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!:\!\mathsf{exit}\{c<0\}
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!:\!x\{\top\}

Figure 15
.

Example where states
q_{1}
and
q_{3}
violate

Send Coherence for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
.

Receive Coherence is conditioned on two simultaneously reachable states
(s_{1},\boldsymbol{r_{1}})
and
(s_{2},\boldsymbol{r_{2}})
for a participant
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}
. It checks that if
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}
can receive
x
from
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
in the first state,
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}
cannot also receive
x
as the first message from
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
in the second state, in which it can also receive from a different participant
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
, unless
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
sending
x
causally depends on
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}
first receiving from
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
. We thus need to define a predicate that captures whether
x_{1}
may be available as the first message from
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}
to
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
, while tracking causal dependencies.
We introduce a family of predicates
\mathsf{avail}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}},\mathcal{B}}(x_{1},s_{2},\boldsymbol{r_{2}})
for this purpose. Here,
\mathcal{B}
is used to track the causal dependencies.
\mathcal{B}
tracks the set of participants that are blocked from sending a message because their send action causally depends on
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}
first receiving from
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
. The predicates are defined as the least fixpoint of the following mutually recursive definition.

Definition 5.5 (Symbolic Availability).

\displaystyle\small\mathsf{avail}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}},\mathcal{B}}(x_{1},s,\boldsymbol{r})\coloneq_{\mu}

\displaystyle\phantom{\lor j}(\bigvee_{\begin{subarray}{c}(s,\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\to{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{t}}}:x\{\varphi\},\,s^{\prime})\in\Delta\\
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\in\mathcal{B}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\neq{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\lor{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{t}}}\neq{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\end{subarray}}\hskip-8.61108pt\exists x~\boldsymbol{r^{\prime}}.\,\mathsf{avail}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}},\mathcal{B}\cup\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{t}}}\}}(x_{1},s^{\prime},\boldsymbol{r^{\prime}})\land\varphi\,)

\displaystyle\lor(\bigvee_{\begin{subarray}{c}(s,\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\to{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{t}}}:x\{\varphi\},\,s^{\prime})\in\Delta\\
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\notin\mathcal{B}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\neq{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\lor{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{t}}}\neq{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\end{subarray}}\hskip-8.61108pt\exists x~\boldsymbol{r^{\prime}}.\,\mathsf{avail}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}},\mathcal{B}}(x_{1},s^{\prime},\boldsymbol{r^{\prime}})\land\varphi\,)\lor(\bigvee_{\begin{subarray}{c}(s,\,{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}:x\{\varphi\},\,s^{\prime})\in\Delta\\
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\notin\mathcal{B}\end{subarray}}\hskip-8.61108pt\varphi[x_{1}/x]\,)\kern 5.0pt.

The last disjunct in the definition handles the cases where the message
x_{1}
from
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
is immediately available to be received by
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}
in symbolic state
(s,\boldsymbol{r})
and
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
has not been blocked from sending. The other two disjuncts handle the cases when
x_{1}
becomes available after some other message exchange between
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{t}}}
. Here, if
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
is blocked, then
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{t}}}
also becomes blocked since it depends on
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
sending before it can receive (the first disjunct). Otherwise, no participant is added to the blocked set (the second disjunct).

With the available message predicate in place, we can now define Symbolic Receive Coherence.

Definition 5.6 (Symbolic Receive Coherence).

A symbolic protocol
\mathbb{S}
satisfies Symbolic Receive Coherence when for every pair of transitions
s_{1}\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}:x_{1}\{\varphi_{1}\}}s_{1}^{\prime}\in\Delta_{1}
and
s_{2}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}:x_{2}\{\varphi_{2}\}}s_{2}^{\prime}\in\Delta_{2}
with
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
:

\mathsf{prodreach}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}(s_{1},\boldsymbol{r_{1}},s_{2},\boldsymbol{r_{2}})~\land~\varphi_{1}~\land~\varphi_{2}~\land~\mathsf{avail}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}},\{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\}}(x_{1},s_{2}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{r_{2}^{\prime}})\implies\bot\kern 5.0pt.

Finally, No Mixed Choice is conditioned on two simultaneously reachable states
(s_{1},\boldsymbol{r_{1}})
and
(s_{2},\boldsymbol{r_{2}})
with outgoing send and receive transitions for a participant
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
.

Definition 5.7 (Symbolic No Mixed Choice).

A symbolic protocol
\mathbb{S}
satisfies Symbolic No Mixed Choice when for every pair of transitions
s_{1}\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}:x_{1}\{\varphi_{1}\}}s_{1}^{\prime}\in\Delta_{1}
and
s_{2}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}:x_{2}\{\varphi_{2}\}}s_{2}^{\prime}\in\Delta_{2}
:

\mathsf{prodreach}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}(s_{1},\boldsymbol{r_{1}},s_{2},\boldsymbol{r_{2}})~\land~\varphi_{1}~\land~\varphi_{2}\implies\bot\kern 5.0pt.

We conclude this section with a discussion of how to check GCLTS assumptions, namely sink finality, sender-driven choice, and deadlock-freedom, on a symbolic protocol.
Sink finality can be checked directly by examining the syntax of the symbolic protocol.
Sender-driven choice without determinism can likewise be checked directly on the states of the symbolic protocol.
Determinism and deadlock-freedom are undecidable in general but can both be reduced to reachability.
Thus, both our Symbolic Coherence Conditions and GCLTS assumptions can be discharged using off-the-shelf
\mu
CLP solvers. We leave such an implementation to future work.

We next apply our framework to decidable fragments of symbolic protocols, some of which have previously been studied in the literature.

5.2.
Finite Protocols

We first consider finite protocols. Let
\mathcal{S}=(S,\Gamma_{\negthinspace\mathit{sync}},T,s_{0},F)
be a protocol with finite
S
and
T
. Because
S
and
T
are finite, we can transform
CC
into an imperative algorithm (see
Algorithm

1
) and use it to check implementability directly. For checking Receive Coherence, we need to decide the predicate
\mathsf{avail}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}},\{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\}}(m,s)
, which is defined like the symbolic availability predicate
\mathsf{avail}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}},\{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\}}(x,s,\boldsymbol{r})
, except on protocols instead of symbolic protocols.

Algorithm 1

Check
CC
for finite protocols

\triangleright

Let LTS
\mathcal{S}=(S,\Gamma_{\negthinspace\mathit{sync}},T,s_{0},F)

\triangleright

Checking Send Coherence

for

s_{1}\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}:m}s_{2}\in T

do

for

s\neq s_{1}\in S

do

if

\mathcal{L}(S,\Gamma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\uplus\{\varepsilon\},T_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}},s_{0},\{s\})\cap\mathcal{L}(S,\Gamma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\uplus\{\varepsilon\},T_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}},s_{0},\{s_{1}\})\neq\emptyset

then

b\leftarrow\bot

for

s_{3}\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}:m}s_{4}\in T

do

b\leftarrow b\lor\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(s\xRightarrow[{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}]{\varepsilon}\mathrel{\vphantom{\to}{}^{*}}s_{3}}}\right)

if

\neg b

then

\textbf{return}~\bot

\triangleright

Checking Receive Coherence

for

s_{1}\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}:m}s_{2},s_{3}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}:m}s_{4}\in T,s_{1}\neq s_{2},{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}

do

if

\mathcal{L}(S,\Gamma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\uplus\{\varepsilon\},T_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}},s_{0},\{s_{1}\})\cap\mathcal{L}(S,\Gamma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\uplus\{\varepsilon\},T_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}},s_{0},\{s_{3}\})\neq\emptyset

then

if

\mathsf{avail}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}},\{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\}}(m,s_{4})

then

\textbf{return}~\bot

\triangleright

Checking No Mixed Choice

for

s_{1}\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}:m}s_{2},s_{3}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}:m}s_{4}\in T,s_{1}\neq s_{2}

do

if

\mathcal{L}(S,\Gamma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\uplus\{\varepsilon\},T_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}},s_{0},\{s_{1}\})\cap\mathcal{L}(S,\Gamma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\uplus\{\varepsilon\},T_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}},s_{0},\{s_{3}\})\neq\emptyset

then

\textbf{return}~\bot

\textbf{return}~\top

It is easy to see that Send Coherence and No Mixed Choice can be checked in time polynomial in the size of
\mathcal{S}
. However, the inclusion of
\mathsf{avail}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}},\{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\}}(m,s)
as a subroutine for checking Receive Coherence yields the following complexity result.

Theorem 5.8.

Implementability of finite protocols is co-NP-complete.

Proof.

To see that implementability is in co-NP, observe that violations of Send Coherence and No Mixed Choice can be checked in NP, by guessing a participant
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
and a pair of states
s_{1},s_{2}
that satisfy the respective preconditions, and verifying simultaneous reachability of
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
for
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
. For Send Coherence, we guess an additional state
s_{3}
with an outgoing transition labeled with
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}:m
, and check
\epsilon
-reachability from
s_{1}
to
s_{3}
.
For Receive Coherence,
\mathsf{avail}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}},\{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\}}(m,s_{2})
can be checked in NP by guessing a simple path in
\mathcal{S}
from
s_{2}
to some state
s^{\prime}
with an outgoing transition labeled with
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}:m
. We then evaluate
\mathsf{avail}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}},\{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\}}(m,s_{2})
along that path, which can be done in polynomial time. We can restrict ourselves to simple paths because the blocked set
\mathcal{B}
monotonically increases when traversing a path in
\mathcal{S}
. Moreover,
\mathsf{avail}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}},\{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\}}(m,s_{2})
is antitone in the blocked set.

We show NP-hardness of non-implementability via a reduction from the 3-SAT problem.
Assume a 3-SAT instance
\varphi=C_{1}\land\ldots\land C_{k}
. Let
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
be the variables occurring in
\varphi
and let
L_{ij}
be the
j
th literal of clause
C_{i}
, with
1\leq i\leq k
and
1\leq j\leq 3
.

We construct a protocol
\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}
over participants
\mathcal{P}=\{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{x_{1}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{\overline{x}_{1}}},\dots,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{x_{n}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{\overline{x}_{n}}}\}
, such that
\varphi
is satisfiable iff
\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}
is implementable.
In particular, we ensure that
\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}
is implementable iff
\mathsf{avail}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}},\{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\}}(m,s)
does not hold for some state
s
in
\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}
.
The protocol
\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}
is constructed from the following subprotocols:

(1)

Define a protocol
\mathcal{S}_{X}
representing a truth assignment to variables
x_{i}
with states
s_{1},\dots,s_{n+1}
as follows: for every
1\leq i\leq n
there are two paths of four transitions each between
s_{i}
and
s_{i+1}
. The paths consist of transitions labeled with
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\to{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{x_{i}}}:\bot
,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\to{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{\overline{x}_{i}}}:\top
,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}:m_{x_{i}}
,
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\to{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{x_{i}}}:m
,
and

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\to{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{\overline{x}_{i}}}:\bot
,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\to{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{x_{i}}}:\top
,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}:m_{\overline{x}_{i}}
,
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\to{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{\overline{x}_{i}}}:m
,
respectively.

(2)

Define a protocol
\mathcal{S}_{C}
representing the clauses
C_{i}
with states
t_{1},\dots,t_{k+1}
as follows. For each
1\leq i\leq k
there are three paths of three transitions between each
t_{i}
and
t_{i+1}
, one for each
1\leq j\leq 3
, labeled with

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\to s:m_{j}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}:m_{r}
,
s\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}:m
, where
s={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{x}}
if
L_{ij}=x
and
s={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{\overline{x}}}
if
L_{ij}=\neg x
for
x\in\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
.

(3)

Define a protocol
\mathcal{S}_{F}
with two states
q_{f}^{\prime}
and
q_{f}
and a single transition from
q_{f}^{\prime}
to
q_{f}
labeled with
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}:m
.

(4)

Define a protocol
\mathcal{S}_{T}
with five states
q_{1},\ldots,q_{5}
, and two paths from
q_{1}
, respectively

q_{1}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}:m_{1}}q_{2}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}:m}q_{3}
and

q_{1}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}:m_{2}}q_{4}\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}:m}q_{5}
.

We merge all of the above protocols to obtain
\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}
by identifying the state
q_{3}
with
s_{1}
,
s_{n+1}
with
t_{1}
and
t_{k+1}
with
q_{f}^{\prime}
. The initial state is
q_{1}
and the final states are
\{q_{5},q_{f}\}
.

Observe that the size of
\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}
is linear in the size of
\varphi
. Moreover, it is easy to check that
\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}
is indeed a GCLTS:
all choices are sender-driven and deterministic, and
final states are the only states with no outgoing transitions,
yielding sink-finality and deadlock-freedom.

We first establish that
\mathsf{avail}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}},\{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\}}(m,q_{3})
holds in
\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}
iff
\varphi
is satisfiable. Observe that the blocked set
\mathcal{B}
computed by
\mathsf{avail}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}},\{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\}}(m,q_{3})
along a path between
s_{1}
and
s_{n+1}
contains for each variable
x_{i}
either
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{x_{i}}}
or
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{\overline{x}_{i}}}
.
The blocked set
\mathcal{B}
thus encodes a truth assignment
\rho_{\mathcal{B}}
for the
x_{i}
’s where
\rho_{\mathcal{B}}(x_{i})=\top
iff
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{x_{i}}}\not\in\mathcal{B}
.
By construction of
\mathcal{S}_{X}
, for every truth assignment
\rho
, there exists a path between
s_{1}
and
s_{n+1}
such that
\rho=\rho_{\mathcal{B}}
for the blocked set
\mathcal{B}
computed along that path.
The paths between states
t_{i}
and
t_{i+1}
in subprotocol
\mathcal{S}_{C}
allow
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
to proceed and not be blocked if one of the paths has a participant not in
\mathcal{B}
, i.e.
C_{i}
is satisfied by
\rho_{\mathcal{B}}
.
Thus, a path from
s_{n+1}=t_{1}
to
t_{k+1}=q_{f}^{\prime}
adds
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
to
\mathcal{B}
at
t_{i}
iff
\rho_{\mathcal{B}}
does not satisfy at least one of the clauses
C_{i}
.
Therefore,
m
is available in
q_{3}
iff there exists a
\mathcal{B}
such that
\rho_{\mathcal{B}}
satisfies
\varphi
.

It remains to show that
\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}
is non-implementable iff
\mathsf{avail}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}},\{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\}}(m,q_{3})
holds in
\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}
.
We argue that all participants except
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}
have sufficient local information about the control flow of the protocol to behave accordingly.
Participant
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
dictates the control flow at every branching point of the protocol, and thus is implementable.
Participants
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{x_{1}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{\overline{x}_{1}}},\ldots{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{x_{n}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{\overline{x}_{n}}}
learn the control flow via receiving messages from participant
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
, whose labels uniquely determine their next actions: receiving
\top
means inaction, receiving
\bot
means receive a further message from
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}
, and receiving
m
means send a message encoding its own variable name to
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
.
Participant
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
is likewise informed by
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
about the control flow, and only sends
m
to
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}
upon either receiving
m_{2}
or
top
from
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
. Upon receiving
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
’s choice of disjunct for each clause, it anticipates a message from the participant encoding that disjunct.

Participant
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}
, on the other hand, is not informed by
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
about
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
’s initial choice at
G_{x_{1}}
, and can locally choose between receptions from
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
or
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
. In the case that
\mathsf{avail}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}},\{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\}}(m,q_{3})
holds, there exists a path from
\overline{G}
to
\mathbf{G}_{\varphi}
in which
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
is not blocked. Thus, the message from
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
can be asynchronously reordered to arrive in
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}
’s channel such that both receptions are enabled, and
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}
may violate implementability by receiving the message from
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
out of order.
If
\mathsf{avail}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}},\{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\}}(m,q_{3})
does not hold, only one reception is enabled, which uniquely informs
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}
about
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
’s choice. In the case that the reception from
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
is enabled,
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}
terminates, otherwise it receives messages from
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
encoding participants to send further messages to, and terminates upon receiving the final message from
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
.
Thus,
\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}
is non-implementable iff
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}
violates Receive Coherence for the transitions

q_{2}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}:m}q_{3}
and

q_{4}\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}:m}q_{5}
, i.e.
\mathsf{avail}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}},\{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\}}(m,q_{3})
does not hold.

We obtain that
\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}
is non-implementable iff
\mathsf{avail}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}},\{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\}}(m,q_{3})
holds in
\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}
iff
\varphi
is satisfiable.
∎

Implementability for global multiparty session types was shown in
(
Li et al., 2023a
)
to be in PSPACE, with the matching lower bound corrected in
(
Li et al., 2023b
)
.
We show that, in fact, the same 3-SAT reduction can be adapted to show co-NP-completeness of implementability for global multiparty session types.

Global multiparty session types

Global types for MSTs

(
Li et al., 2023a
)
are defined by the grammar:

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle\Coloneqq 0\mid\sum_{i∈I}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}_{i}\!:\!m_{i}.G_{i}\mid\mu t.\;G\mid t

where
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}_{i}
range over
\mathcal{P}
,
m_{i}
over a finite set
\mathcal{V}
, and
t
over a set of recursion variables.
The semantics of a global type
\mathbf{G}
are defined using a finite state machine

\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})=(Q_{\mathbf{G}},\Gamma_{sync}\uplus\{\varepsilon\},δ_{\mathbf{G}},q_{0,\mathbf{G}},F_{\mathbf{G}})

where

Q_{\mathbf{G}}
is the set of all syntactic subterms in
\mathbf{G}
together with the term
0
,

δ_{\mathbf{G}}
is the smallest set containing

(\sum_{i∈I}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}_{i}\!:\!m_{i}.G_{i},{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}_{i}\!:\!m_{i},G_{i})
for each
i∈I
,
as well as
(μt.G^{\prime},ε,G^{\prime})
and
(t,ε,μt.G^{\prime})
for each subterm
\mu t.G^{\prime}
,

q_{0,\mathbf{G}}=\mathbf{G}
and

F_{\mathbf{G}}=\{0\}
.

Each branch of a choice is assumed to be distinct:

∀i,j∈I.\,i≠j⇒({{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}_{i},m_{i})≠({{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}_{j},m_{j})
,
and the sender and receiver of an atomic action is assumed to be distinct:

∀i∈I.\,{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}≠{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}_{i}
.
Recursion is guarded:
in
μt.\,G
, there is at least one message between
μt
and each
t
in
G
.

Each
\varepsilon
-transition in
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
is the only transition from the state it originates from.
This makes removing them easy, yielding a protocol
\mathcal{S_{\mathbf{G}}}=(Q_{\mathbf{G}},\Gamma_{\negthinspace\mathit{sync}},\delta^{\prime}_{\mathbf{G}},q_{0,\mathbf{G}},F_{\mathbf{G}})
, where
\delta^{\prime}_{\mathbf{G}}
contains only transitions labeled with
l\in\Gamma_{\negthinspace\mathit{sync}}
.
It is easy to verify that
\mathcal{S_{\mathbf{G}}}
is indeed a GCLTS.

Lemma 5.9.

Implementability of global types is co-NP-complete.

The proof can be found in
Appendix

B
.

5.3.
Symbolic Finite Protocols

Finally, we study symbolic representations of finite protocols. More precisely, we consider the fragment of symbolic protocols where
\mathcal{V}
is the set of Booleans and all transition constraints
\varphi
are given by propositional formulas.
We show that for this class of symbolic protocols, the implementability problem is PSPACE-complete.

Theorem 5.10.

Implementability of symbolic finite protocols is PSPACE-complete.

Proof sketch.

To show that implementability is in PSPACE, we show that a witness to the negation of
CC
can be checked in nondeterministic polynomial space. This follows by a reduction to the reachability problem for extended finite state machines, which is in PSPACE
(
Godefroid and Yannakakis, 2013
)
. By Savitch’s Theorem, it follows that the negation of
CC
is in PSPACE. Because PSPACE is closed under complement and
CC
precisely characterizes implementability, it follows that implementability is in PSPACE.

We show PSPACE-hardness of the implementability problem by a reduction from the PSPACE-hard problem of deciding reachability for 1-safe Petri nets
(
Esparza and Nielsen, 1994
)
.
Let
(N,M_{0})
be a 1-safe Petri net, with
N=(S,T,F)
.

Let
M
be a marking of
N
.
We construct a symbolic protocol that is implementable iff
N
does not reach
M
. For ease of exposition, we present this symbolic protocol as a symbolic dependent global type
\mathbf{G}_{N}
with the understanding that the encoding of
\mathbf{G}_{N}
as a symbolic protocol is clear.

We first describe the construction of
\mathbf{G}_{N}
.
The outermost structure of
\mathbf{G}_{N}
consists of a participant
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
communicating a choice between two branches to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}

where the bottom branch solely consists of
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
sending
l
to
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}
:

\mathbf{G}_{N}\coloneq({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}\!:\!m_{1}\{\top\}.\,G_{t}+{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}\!:\!m_{2}\{\top\}.\,{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!:\!l\{\top\}.\,0)
.
Since
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
is not informed about the choice of the branch taken by
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}
, it will have to be able to match this send transition in every run that follows the continuation
G_{t}
of the top branch. We will construct
G_{t}
such that this match is possible iff
M
is reachable in
N
.
In
G_{t}
, participants
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}
enter a loop that simulates
N
:

\small G_{t}\coloneq\mu s[v\coloneq M_{0}].\,+\begin{cases}\sum_{t\in T}\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}\!:\!m_{t}\{v\Rightarrow t^{-}\}.\,s[v\coloneq((v\land\neg t^{-})\lor t^{+})]\\
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}\!:\!\mathit{restart}\{\top\}.\,s[v\coloneq M_{0}]\\
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}\!:\!\mathit{reach}_{M}\{v=M\}.\,{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!:\!l\{\top\}.\,0\\
\end{cases}

The loop variable
v
is a
\lvert S\rvert
-length bitvector that tracks the current marking of the net. It is initialized to
M_{0}
.
Inside the loop,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
has the following choices. First, it may pick any transition
t\in T
of the net and send an
m_{t}
message to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}
, provided the transition is enabled for firing (i.e., the input places of
t
all contain a token:
v\Rightarrow t^{-}
). After this communication,
v
is updated according to the fired transition
t
.
The last branch of the choice in the loop is enabled if
v
is equal to
M
. Here,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
can send
\mathit{reach}_{M}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}
, which gives
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
the opportunity to send the
l
message to
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}
, allowing it to match the send transition from the lower branch in the top level choice of
G_{N}
.
Finally, the middle branch allows
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
to abort the simulation at any point and start over. This ensures that if the simulation ever reaches a dead state due to firing a transition that would render
M
unreachable, it can recover by starting again from
M_{0}
. Thus, for all states of the simulator,
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
has an
\varepsilon
path from that state to a state where it can send
l
to
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}
iff
M
is reachable from
M_{0}
in
N
. The only other sender is
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
which makes all choices and, hence, never reaches two different states along the same prefix trace, thus satisfying Send Coherence trivially. It follows that Send Coherence for
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
holds iff
M
is reachable from
M_{0}
in
N
. To see that Receive Coherence holds, observe that no participant receives messages from two different senders. No Mixed Choice similarly holds trivially.

G_{N}
is deadlock-free because the branch in the loop of
G_{t}
where
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
sends the
\mathit{restart}
message is always enabled. Moreover, it is easy to see that
G_{N}
is deterministic because each branch of a choice sends a different message value.
In summary,
G_{N}
is a GCLTS that is implementable iff
N
reaches
M
. The size of
\mathbf{G}_{N}
is linear in the size of
N
, so we obtain the desired reduction.

∎

6.

Table 1.
Comparison of related work (in chronological order)

Table

1
summarizes the most closely related works that address the implementability problem of communication protocols with data refinements.
We discuss these works in terms of key expressive features and completeness of characterization.

Expressivity.

All existing works in
Table

1
effectively require
history-sensitivity
, which means that a
“predicate guaranteed by a [participant
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
] can only contain those interaction variables that [
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
] knows”
(
Bocchi et al., 2010
)
, see also
(
Bocchi et al., 2012
, Def. 2)
.
As discussed in §
4
, syntactic approaches to analyzing variable knowledge is overly conservative, and as a result no prior work can handle protocols such as the example in
Fig.

12
.
In a similar vein,
Zhou et al. (2020)
impose the syntactic restriction that all participants in a loop must be able to update all loop registers, which rules out loops like the one in the two-bidder protocol (
Fig.

2
).
Furthermore, all prior works except for

(
Gheri et al., 2022
)

employ the directed choice restriction, which is strictly less general than sender-driven choice.
Many of these works also feature separate constructs for selecting branches and sending data.
In our symbolic protocols, this is not necessary because selecting branches can be modeled with equality predicates, as demonstrated by
Fig.

7
.

Gheri et al. (2022)
generalize choreography automata, which are finite-state LTSs with communciation events as transition labels but without final states.
One major difference between our work and theirs lies in the treatment of interleavings.
Unlike our protocol semantics, which are closed under the indistinguishability relation
\sim
, inspired by Lamport’s happened-before relation, choreography automata languages do not include any interleavings not present in the language.
Setting aside asynchronous traces, the protocol

{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!:\!m.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}\!:\!m.\,0

in our setting would need to be represented as

{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!:\!m.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}\!:\!m.\,0\;+\;{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}\!:\!m.\,{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!:\!m.\,0

in their setting, and the protocol

\mu t.\,{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!:\!m.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}\!:\!m.\,t

does not admit a representation as a choreography automaton.
The branching behaviors are restricted with a well-sequencedness condition
(
Gheri et al., 2022
, Def. 3.2)
, a condition that has since been refined because it was shown to be flawed
(
Finkel and Lozes, 2023
)
.

Majumdar et al. (2021b)
showed that well-formedness conditions on synchronous choreography automata do not generalize soundly to the asynchronous setting.
Asynchronous communication is more challenging to analyze in general because it easily gives rise to infinite-state systems.

Zhou (2024)
conjectures that the framework in

(
Zhou et al., 2020
)
“can be extended to support asynchronous communication”,
but does not conjecture if and how the projection operator would change.
Due to directed choice, the same projection operator may remain sound under asynchronous semantics, because it rules out protocols where participants have a choice to receive from different senders.
However, it will also likely inherit the same sources of incompleteness present in the synchronous setting.
In contrast to all aforementioned works,
several works
(
Dagnino et al., 2021
;
Castellani et al., 2022
;
Castellani et al., 2024
)
allow to specify send and receive events separately with “deconfined” global types.
Deconfined global types are specified as a parallel composition of local processes, and then checked for desirable correctness properties, which were shown to be undecidable
(
Dagnino et al., 2021
)
.

Completeness.

Implementability is a thoroughly-studied problem in the high-level message sequence chart (HMSC) literature.
HMSCs are a standardized formalism for describing communication protocols in industry
(
Union, 1996
)
and are well-studied in academia

(
Mauw and Reniers, 1997
;
Genest et al., 2003
;
Genest and Muscholl, 2005
;
Gazagnaire et al., 2007
;
Roychoudhury et al., 2012
)
.
In the HMSC setting, implementability is called safe realizability, and is defined with respect to the implementation model of communicating finite state machines
(
Brand and Zafiropulo, 1983
)
.
Similar to our setting, a canonical implementation exists for any HMSC
(
Alur et al., 2003
, Thm. 13)
; unlike our setting, it is always computable.
Therefore, existing work has focused less on synthesis and more on checking implementability.
Despite having only finite states and data, HMSC implementability was shown to be undecidable in general
(
Lohrey, 2003
)
.
Various fragments have since been identified in which the problem regains decidability.

Lohrey (2003)
showed implementability to be EXPSPACE-complete for bounded HMSCs
(
Alur and Yannakakis, 1999
;
Muscholl and Peled, 1999
)
and globally-cooperative HMSCs
(
Morin, 2002
;
Genest et al., 2006
)
.
These fragments restrict the communication topology of loops to be strongly and weakly connected respectively.
For HMSCs where every two consecutive communications share a participant, implementability was shown to be PSPACE-complete
(
Lohrey, 2003
)
.
In contrast, works that study comparably expressive protocol fragments to ours often sidestep the implementability question.
Instead, implementability is addressed in the form of syntactic well-formedness conditions, as mentioned above, or indirectly through synthesis.
None of the prior works attempted to show completeness; it was later shown in
(
Stutz, 2023
;
Li et al., 2023a
)
that all but
Gheri et al. (2022)
are incomplete.
Several works
(
Bocchi et al., 2010
;
Bocchi et al., 2012
;
Toninho and Yoshida, 2017
;
Zhou et al., 2020
)
synthesize local implementations using the “classical” projection from multiparty session types.
One kind of merge operator, called the plain merge, allows only the two participants in a choice to exhibit different behavior on each branch, a condition which is breached by our two-bidder protocol
(
Fig.

2
).

Zhou et al. (2020)
proves the soundness of projection with plain merge, but implements a more permissive variant called full merge in the toolchain.
However, the projected local types are not guaranteed to be implementable:
both
Fig.

12
and
Fig.

12
are projectable in
(
Zhou et al., 2020
)
.
Thus, the implementability problem is deferred to local types.
Our results show that synthesis is “as possible as” the determinization of the non-deterministic underlying automata fragment.
This means that implementations can be synthesized even for expressive classes of protocols that correspond to e.g. symbolic finite automata
(
D’Antoni and Veanes, 2017
;
Shen et al., 2023
)
and certain classes of timed and register automata
(
Bertrand et al., 2015
;
Clemente et al., 2022
)
due to the existence of off-the-shelf determinization algorithms for these classes
(
Veanes et al., 2010
;
Veanes and Bjørner, 2012
;
Bertrand et al., 2018
)
.

Scalas and Yoshida (2019)
check safety properties of collections of local types by encoding the properties as
\mu
-calculus formulas and then model checking the typing context against the specification. They focus primarily on finite-state typing contexts under synchronous semantics, and thus all properties in their setting are decidable. For the asynchronous setting, only three sound approximations of safety are proposed, one of which bounds channel sizes and thus falls back into the finite-state setting.

Next, we discuss further related works on choreographic programming and binary session types.

Choreographic programming.

Choreographic programming
(
Cruz-Filipe and Montesi, 2020
;
Giallorenzo et al., 2021
;
Hirsch and Garg, 2022
)
describes global message-passing behaviors as programs rather than protocols, and therefore incorporate many more programming language features that are abstracted away in our model, such as computation and mutable state, in addition to features that our model cannot express, such as higher-order computations and delegation.
Endpoint projection for choreographic programs, which shares a theoretical basis with multiparty session type projection, then generates individual, executable programs for each participant.
The question of implementability, though undecidable in the presence of such expressivity, remains relevant to the soundness of endpoint projections.
We discuss three approaches to endpoint projection.
Pirouette
(
Hirsch and Garg, 2021
)
requires the programmer to specify explicit synchronization messages to ensure that “different locations stay in lock-step with each other”, and conservatively rejects programs that are underspecified in this regard. Pirouette provides a mechanized proof of deadlock freedom for endpoint projections in Coq.
Note that the claims of soundness and completeness in
(
Hirsch and Garg, 2021
)
are not with respect to implementability, but with respect to the translation via endpoint projection.
HasChor
(
Shen et al., 2023
)
rules out non-implementability by automatically incorporating location broadcasts when a choice is made.
No formal correctness claims are made in
(
Shen et al., 2023
)
.

Jongmans and van den Bos (2022)
allow if- and while- statements to be annotated with a conjunction of conditional choices for each participant, which expresses decentralized decision-making in protocols.
They show that their endpoint projection for well-formed choreographies guarantees deadlock freedom and functional correctness.
All aforementioned choreographic programming works assume a synchronous network.

Binary session types with refinements.

Finally, we briefly mention work on binary session types with refinements and data dependencies.
In the binary setting, implementability is a less interesting problem due to the inherent duality between the two protocol participants; the distinction between global and local types is no longer meaningful.

Griffith and Gunter (2013)
refine binary sessions with basic data types, and shows decidability of the subtyping problem.

Gommerstadt et al. (2018)
applies a similar type system for runtime monitoring of binary communication.

Thiemann and Vasconcelos (2020)
propose a label-dependent binary session type framework which allows the subsequent behavior of the protocol to depend on previous labels, which are drawn from a finite set.

Das and Pfenning (2020)
study the undecidable problem of local type equality, and provide a sound approximate algorithm.
Das et al.
(
Das and Pfenning, 2022
;
Das et al., 2021
)
further apply binary session types with refinements to resource analysis of blockchain smart contracts and amortized cost analysis.
Actris
(
Hinrichsen et al., 2020
)
embeds binary session types into the Iris framework
(
Jung et al., 2018
)
.
The framework assumes asynchronous communication with FIFO channels, and can verify programs that combine message-passing concurrency and shared-memory concurrency.
Actris has been extended with session type subtyping (Actris 2.0
(
Hinrichsen et al., 2022
)
) and with linearity to prove both preservation and progress (LinearActris
(
Jacobs et al., 2024
)
).
Multris
(
Hinrichsen et al., 2024
)
is an extension of Actris in Iris to the multiparty setting.
The message-passing layer of Multris is more restricted than Actris: Multris assumes synchronous communication and prohibits choice over channels: choices can only be made about message values between a given sender and receiver.
Multris takes a bottom-up approach
(
Scalas and Yoshida, 2019
)
to correctness: given a collection of local types, the type system checks that they can be safely combined.
Multris guarantees protocol fidelity but not progress.

Proof.

Let
\rho
be a run in
I(w)
, and let

w^{\prime}=\texttt{split}(\texttt{trace}(\rho))\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S})
.
In the case that
I(w)
contains finite runs, we can pick a finite
\rho
. Otherwise,
\rho
is infinite.
We reason about each case in turn.

Case:
\rho
is a finite run.

In the case that
\rho
is a finite run, to show that

w\in\operatorname{pref}(\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}))

we need to show the existence of a
w^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S})
such that
w\leq w^{\prime\prime}
.
We construct such a
w^{\prime\prime}
by constructing a
u
such that in
wu
, all participants have completed their actions in
\rho
, and furthermore
wu
is channel-compliant.
Then, because
w^{\prime}
is channel-compliant by construction, and for all participants
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}
, it holds that
wu{\Downarrow}_{Σ_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}=w^{\prime}{\Downarrow}_{Σ_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
, by
(
Majumdar et al., 2021a
, Lemma 23)
it follows that
wu\sim w^{\prime}
, and thus
wu\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S})
.

For each participant
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}
, let
y_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
be defined such that

w{\Downarrow}_{Σ_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\cdot y_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}=w^{\prime}{\Downarrow}_{Σ_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
.
We construct
u
from the
y_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
for each participant, starting with
u=\varepsilon
.
If there exists some participant in
\mathcal{P}
such that
y_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}[0]\in Σ_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}},!}
, append
y_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
to
u
and update
y_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
.
If not, for all participants
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}
,
y_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}[0]\in Σ_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}},?}
.
Each symbol
y_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}[0]
for all participants appears in
v
.
Let
i_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
denote for each participant the index in
w^{\prime}
such that
w^{\prime}[i]=y_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}[0]
.
Let
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
be the participant with the minimum index
i_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
: append
y_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
to
u
and update
y_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}
.
Termination is guaranteed by the strictly decreasing measure of
\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}}|y_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}|
.
Furthermore, it is clear that upon termination, for all participants
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}
,

wu{\Downarrow}_{Σ_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}=w^{\prime}{\Downarrow}_{Σ_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
.

We argue that
wu
satisfies the inductive invariant of channel compliancy.
In the case where
u
is extended with a send action, channel compliancy is trivially re-established.
In the receive case, channel compliancy is re-established by the fact that the append order for receive actions follows that in
v
, which is channel-compliant by construction.

Case:
\rho
is an infinite run.

In the case that
\rho
is a infinite run, to show that

w\in\operatorname{pref}(\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}))

we likewise need to show the existence of a
w^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S})
such that
w\leq w^{\prime\prime}
.
Like before, we construct a
u
and show that
wu\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S})
.
However, unlike before, we cannot rely on the fact that
wu\sim w^{\prime}
to show that
wu\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S})
, because
w^{\prime}
is an infinite word and
(
Majumdar et al., 2021a
, Lemma 23)
applies only to finite words.
Instead, we must prove that
wu\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S})
by the definition of infinite word membership in
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S})
, namely:

wu\preceq_{\sim}^{\omega}w^{\prime}
.
By the definition of
\preceq_{\sim}^{\omega}
, it further suffices to show that:

\forall v\leq wu,~\exists v^{\prime}\leq w^{\prime},u^{\prime}\in Σ_{\mathit{async}}^{*}.~vu^{\prime}\sim v^{\prime}\kern 5.0pt.

For each participant
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}
, let
\rho_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
be defined as the largest prefix of
\rho
such that

\texttt{split}(\texttt{trace}(\rho_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}})){\Downarrow}_{Σ_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}=w{\Downarrow}_{Σ_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
Let
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}
be the participant with the maximum
|\rho_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}|
in
\mathcal{P}
.
Clearly,
\rho_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}\leq\rho
.
Let
\beta
be defined such that
\rho=\rho_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}\cdot\beta
.
We split the construction of
u
into two parts: let
u=u_{1}u_{2}
.
We construct
u_{1}
as above, by appending uncompleted actions in
\rho_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}
, ordering send events before receive events, and further ordering receive events by the order in which they appear in
\rho_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}
.
Then, upon termination,
wu_{1}
is channel-compliant and satisfies for all
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}
,

wu_{1}{\Downarrow}_{Σ_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}=\texttt{split}(\texttt{trace}(\rho_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}})){\Downarrow}_{Σ_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
.
Let
u_{2}=\texttt{split}(\texttt{trace}(\beta))
.

We now show that
wu_{1}u_{2}\preceq_{\sim}^{\omega}w^{\prime}
.

Let
v
be an arbitrary prefix of
wu_{1}u_{2}
.
If
v\leq wu_{1}
, we pick
v^{\prime}=\texttt{split}(\texttt{trace}(\rho_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}))\leq w^{\prime}
and
u^{\prime}\in Σ_{\mathit{async}}*
to be such that
vu^{\prime}=wu_{1}
.
Otherwise, if
wu_{1}<v
, let
\rho^{\prime}
be defined as the smallest prefix of
\rho
such that for all participants
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}
,

v{\Downarrow}_{Σ_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}=\texttt{split}(\texttt{trace}(\rho^{\prime})){\Downarrow}_{Σ_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
.
We pick
v^{\prime}=\texttt{split}(\texttt{trace}(\rho^{\prime}))
.
Because
v
is channel-compliant, we can repeat the reasoning in the finite case to extend
v
with
u^{\prime}
and apply
(
Majumdar et al., 2021a
, Lemma 23)
to conclude that
vu^{\prime}\sim v^{\prime}
.
∎

Claim 1.

\operatorname{pref}(\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}))\subseteq\operatorname{pref}(\mathcal{L}(\{\!\!\{T_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\}\!\!\}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}}))

From the induction hypothesis,

.

The fact that

In the case that

This follows from the fact that
w

This concludes our proof of prefix set inclusion.

Case:

are final. From the fact that all finite words in

contain matching receive events, all channels in

are empty.

Case:

w\in Σ_{\mathit{async}}^{\omega}
.
The infinite case when
w\in Σ_{\mathit{async}}^{\omega}

Proof.

Let
w\in\mathcal{L}\{\!\!\{T_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\}\!\!\}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
.
We again case split on whether
w
is a finite or infinite word.

Case:

w\in Σ^{*}
.
First, we establish that
w
is terminated.
Let
(\vec{s},\xi)
be the
\{\!\!\{T_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\}\!\!\}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
configuration reached on
w
.
Because
w
is a finite, maximal word in
\mathcal{L}(\{\!\!\{T_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\}\!\!\}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}})
, it holds that all states in
\vec{s}
are final, and all channels in
\xi
are empty. Therefore, no receive transitions are enabled from
(\vec{s},\xi)
.
We argue that no send transitions are enabled from
(\vec{s},\xi)
either.
Suppose by contradiction that there exists an outgoing transition
\vec{s}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}!m}s^{\prime}\in T_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
for participant
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
.
Then,
w{\Downarrow}_{Σ_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\cdot{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}!m\in\operatorname{pref}(\mathcal{L}(T_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}))
, and by the canonicity of
T_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
,

w{\Downarrow}_{Σ_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\cdot{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}!m\in\operatorname{pref}(\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S})){\Downarrow}_{Σ_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
.
Then, there exists a maximal run
\rho^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{S}
such that

w{\Downarrow}_{Σ_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\cdot{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}!m\leq\texttt{split}(\texttt{trace}(\rho^{\prime})){\Downarrow}_{Σ_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
.
Furthermore, there exists a finite, maximal run
\rho_{fin}
in
\mathcal{S}
such that

w{\Downarrow}_{Σ_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}=\texttt{split}(\texttt{trace}(\rho)){\Downarrow}_{Σ_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
.
Let
s_{fin}
be the last state in
\rho_{fin}
. By assumption,
s_{fin}\in F
.
Let
\alpha\cdot s_{1}\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}:m}s_{2}\cdot\beta
be the unique splitting of
\rho^{\prime}
for
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
with respect to
w
.
Then,
s_{1}
and
s_{fin}
are simultaneously reachable for
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
on prefix
w{\Downarrow}_{Σ_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
.
From SC, there exists a
s_{2}^{\prime}
such that
s_{fin}\xRightarrow[{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}]{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}:m}\mathrel{\vphantom{\to}{}^{*}}s_{2}^{\prime}
.
We find a contradiction to the assumption that final states in
\mathcal{S}
do not have outgoing transitions.

Next, we show that for every
\rho\in I(w)
, it holds that for every
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}
,

w{\Downarrow}_{Σ_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}=\texttt{split}(\texttt{trace}(\rho)){\Downarrow}_{Σ_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
.
This implies that there exist no infinite runs in
I(w)
.
Suppose by contradiction that there exists a run
\rho\in I(w)
and a non-empty set of participants
\mathcal{Q}
such that for every
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}\in\mathcal{Q}
, it holds that

w{\Downarrow}_{Σ_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}<\bigl(\texttt{split}(\texttt{trace}(\rho))\bigr){\Downarrow}_{Σ_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
(*).

Given a participant
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
, let
\rho_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
denote the largest prefix of
\rho
that contains
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
’s local view of
w
. Formally,

\rho_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}=max\{\rho^{\prime}~|~\rho^{\prime}\leq\rho~\land~\texttt{split}(\texttt{trace}(\rho^{\prime})){\Downarrow}_{Σ_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}=w{\Downarrow}_{Σ_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\kern 5.0pt.

Note that due to maximality, the next transition in
\rho
after
\rho_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
must have
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
as its active participant.
Let
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}
be the participant in
\mathcal{S}
for whom
\rho_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}
is the smallest.
From the canonicity of
T_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}
and (*), it follows that
\vec{s}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
has outgoing transitions.
If
\vec{s}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
has outgoing send transitions, then we reach a contradiction to the fact that
w
is terminated.
If
\vec{s}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
has outgoing receive transitions, it must be the case that the next transition in
\rho
after
\rho_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}
is of the form
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\!\to\!{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}\!:\!m
for some
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
and
m
.
From the fact that
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}
is the participant with the smallest
\rho_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}
, we know that
\rho_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}<\rho_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
, and from the FIFO property of CLTS channels it follows that
m
is in
\xi({{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}})
.
Then, the receive transition is enabled for
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}
, and we again reach a contradiction to the fact that
w
is terminated.

Thus, we can pick any finite run
\rho\in I(w)
which is maximal by definition, and invoke
(
Majumdar et al., 2021a
, Lemma 23)
to conclude that
\texttt{split}(\texttt{trace}(\rho))\sim w
, and thus
w\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S})
.

Case:

w\in Σ^{\infty}
.
By the semantics of
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S})
, to show
w\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S})
it suffices to show:

\exists w^{\prime}\in Σ^{\omega}.~w^{\prime}\in\texttt{split}(\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}))\land w\preceq_{\sim}^{\omega}w^{\prime}\kern 5.0pt.

Claim.

\bigcap_{u\leq w}I(u)
contains an infinite run.

First, we show that there exists an infinite run in
\mathcal{S}
.
We apply König’s Lemma to an infinite tree where each vertex corresponds to a finite run.
We obtain the vertex set from the intersection sets of
w
’s prefixes; each prefix “contributes” a set of finite runs.
Formally, for each prefix
u\leq w
, let
V_{u}
be defined as:

V_{u}\coloneq\bigcup_{\rho_{u}\in I(u)}\text{min}\{\rho^{\prime}\mid\rho^{\prime}\leq\rho_{u}\land\forall{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}.~u{\Downarrow}_{Σ_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\leq\texttt{split}(\texttt{trace}(\rho^{\prime})){\Downarrow}_{Σ_{{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\}\kern 5.0pt.

By the assumption that
I(u)\neq\emptyset
,
V_{u}
is guaranteed to be non-empty.
We construct a tree
\mathcal{T}_{w}(V,E)
with

V\coloneq\bigcup_{u\leq w}V_{u}
and

E\coloneq\{(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})\mid\rho_{1}\leq\rho_{2}\}
.
The tree is rooted in the empty run, which is included in
V
by the prefix
\varepsilon
.

V
is infinite because there are infinitely many prefixes of
w
.

\mathcal{T}_{w}
is finitely branching due to the fact that
\mathcal{S}
is deterministic: while there can be infinitely many transitions from a given state in
S
, there are only finitely many transitions from a given state in
S
on a particular transition label. In fact, there is only a single transition.
Therefore, we can apply König’s Lemma to obtain a ray in
\mathcal{T}_{w}
representing an infinite run in
\mathcal{S}
.

Let
\rho^{\prime}
be such an infinite run.
We now show that
\rho^{\prime}\in\bigcap_{u\leq w}I(u)
.
Let
v
be a prefix of
w
.
To show that
\rho^{\prime}\in I(v)
, it suffices to show that one of the vertices in
V_{v}
lies on
\rho^{\prime}
. In other words,

V_{v}\cap\{v\mid v\in\rho^{\prime}\}\neq\emptyset\kern 5.0pt.

Assume by contradiction that
\rho^{\prime}
passes through none of the vertices in
V_{v}
.
Then, for any
u^{\prime}\geq u
, because intersection sets are monotonically decreasing, it must be the case that
\rho^{\prime}
passes through none of the vertices in
V_{u}^{\prime}
.
Therefore,
\rho^{\prime}
can only pass through vertices in
V_{u}^{\prime\prime}
, where
u^{\prime\prime}\leq u
.
However, the set
\bigcup_{u^{\prime\prime}\leq u}V_{u}^{\prime\prime}
has finite cardinality.
We reach a contradiction, concluding our proof of the above claim.

Let
\rho^{\prime}\in\bigcap_{u\leq w}I(u)
, and let

w^{\prime}=\texttt{split}(\texttt{trace}(\rho^{\prime}))
.
It is clear that

w^{\prime}\in Σ_{\mathit{async}}^{\omega}
and
w^{\prime}\in\texttt{split}(\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}))
.
It remains to show that

w\preceq_{\sim}^{\omega}w^{\prime}
.
By the definition of
\preceq_{\sim}^{\omega}
, it further suffices to show that:

\forall u\leq w,~\exists u^{\prime}\leq w^{\prime},v\in Σ_{\mathit{async}}^{*}.~uv\sim u^{\prime}\kern 5.0pt.

Let
u
be an arbitrary prefix of
w
.
Because by definition
\rho^{\prime}\in I(u)
, it holds that

u{\Downarrow}_{Σ_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\leq\texttt{split}(\texttt{trace}(\rho^{\prime})){\Downarrow}_{Σ_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.

For each participant
{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}\in\mathcal{P}
, let
\rho^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}
be defined as the largest prefix of
\rho^{\prime}
such that

\texttt{split}(\texttt{trace}(\rho^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}})){\Downarrow}_{Σ_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}=u{\Downarrow}_{Σ_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
Such a run is well-defined by the fact that
u
is a prefix of an infinite word
w
